- أدوية مفتاحية: غليكوبيررونيوم 200–400 ميكروغرام SC، ميدازولام 2.5–5 ملغ SC كبداية، استخدم الأفيونات لِتخفيف وطأة ضيق التنفُّس عند توفر الأوكسجين. الإجراءات الغازية فقط إن تتفق مع أهداف المريض. رتب متابعة مع فريق الرعاية الملطفة.

6. High‑yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- Pitfalls:
  - Don’t give oxygen reflexively for breathlessness if SpO2 adequate; oxygen often does not relieve subjective dyspnoea. (NICE CKS)
  - Do not routinely suction terminal secretions — antimuscarinics are preferred.
  - Avoid prokinetic antiemetics in high‑grade mechanical bowel obstruction.
  - Confirm validity of advance decisions/DNACPR before withholding interventions; legal frameworks differ by jurisdiction (England specifics per NHS England 2017).
  - Benzodiazepines may worsen respiratory drive — use cautiously; opioids for breathlessness are evidence‑based when titrated carefully.
- Exam tricks:
  - If SpO2 low → oxygen is first-line; if SpO2 normal but breathlessness persists → opioid is typical correct choice.
  - “Death rattle” scenario + good oxygenation + clear chest → antimuscarinic (glycopyrronium/hyoscine) is frequently the tested answer.
  - In nausea with raised ICP/vestibular signs → cyclizine preferred in many exam stems.
  - For agitation due to reversible causes — always choose treat reversible cause first (pain, hypoxia, urinary retention) before sedation answers.
  - When question mentions advance decision refusing life‑sustaining treatment — choose comfort measures consistent with that decision.

شرح موجز بالعربية:
- أخطاء شائعة: لا تعطِ أكسجين لو تشبّع كافٍ؛ لا تمسح الإفرازات الروتينية؛ تحقق من صلاحية القرارات المسبقة. نصيحة امتحانيّة: «مرفين عند ضيق تنفّس متبقٍ ولوكسجين كافٍ» غالبًا صحيحة.

7. Arabic rapid‑review summary (ملخص عربي سريع)
- أهداف: راحة، التحكم بالاعراض، احترام رغبات المريض. وثّق خطة العلاج.  
- ضيق التنفّس: أكسجين إن SpO2 ≤92%؛ إن تشبع كافٍ → مرفين منخفض الجرعة؛ إذا قلق → بنزوديازيبين مضاد للقلق.  
- أنفاس صاخبة: غالبًا إفرازات نهائية → غليكوبيررونيوم أو هيوسين؛ لا شفط روتيني.  
- هيجان/هلع نهائي: اسْتبعد مسببات قابلة للعلاج ثم استخدم ميدازولام بحسب الحاجة.  
- غثيان: سيكلزين إذا مشكلة دوُريّة/ضغط دماغي؛ تجنّب محفزات الحركة لو انسداد ميكانيكى.  
- ألم عصبي: علاج تكميلي (جابابينتويد/مضاد اكتئاب) يضاف للأفيونات.  
- فحوصات: اطلب فقط إن ستغيّر إدارة المريض.  
- قانونيًا: تحقق من صحة القرارات المسبقة/DNACPR قبل وقف علاجات منقذة للحياة.

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- Dyspnoea: recognition, causes, SpO2 threshold, oxygen vs opioid management, anxiety component.
- Noisy breathing / “death rattle”: recognition, antimuscarinics vs suction.
- Agitation/delirium at end of life: reversible causes, midazolam, antipsychotics.
- Nausea/vomiting in palliative patients: ICP/vestibular vs bowel obstruction vs chemo/post‑op; drug choices (cyclizine, hyoscine, ondansetron).
- Pain management: opioid titration, neuropathic adjuvants.
- Procedural decisions in palliative context: when to drain effusions, paracentesis, bowel decompression; aligning with goals.
- Legal/ethical: advance decisions to refuse treatment, DNACPR, documentation and verification.
- Diagnostics: focused bedside assessment, selective use of CXR/US/ABG/bloods.
- Common exam vignettes/tricks: mapping SpO2→oxygen vs opioid, death rattle→antimuscarinic, nausea mechanism→antiemetic class.

This chapter is study material, not patient‑specific advice.

Sources to verify: RCEM, ERC, NICE CKS (palliative care: dyspnoea), NICE guidance, BNF (palliative prescribing), NHS England — Advance Decisions to Refuse Treatment (2017), local palliative care protocols and legal guidance.


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 15: OPHTHALMOLOGY
============================================================================================

Chapter 15 — Ophthalmology (compact FRCEM final chapter)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Triage principle: identify vision- or globe‑threatening features early — new ↓vision, severe/boring pain, photophobia, corneal opacity/ulcer, hypopyon, fixed/mid‑dilated pupil, very high IOP, proptosis/tight orbit, RAPD. Act immediately if present. [Eyewiki; NICE CKS]
- Major pathophysiologic clusters:
  - Surface infections/ulceration → corneal epithelium breach → risk of stromal invasion, hypopyon, endophthalmitis.
  - Angle closure → sudden blockage of trabecular outflow → rapid IOP rise → optic nerve/retinal ischemia.
  - Orbital compartment syndrome (OCS)/retrobulbar haemorrhage → raised orbital pressure → globe ischemia and optic nerve compression.
  - Penetrating injury/open globe → loss of globe integrity → infection risk, expulsive hemorrhage.
  - Vascular occlusions (CRAO/CRVO) → retinal ischemia or venous congestion → acute painless vision loss.
  - Inflammatory/autoimmune (scleritis, GCA) → deep pain, scleral necrosis or anterior ischemic optic neuropathy.
- Time-critical events: OCS (immediate canthotomy/cantholysis), AACG (rapid IOP lowering + ophth), endophthalmitis (intravitreal antibiotics), open globe (shield + urgent ophth). [Eyewiki; NCBI]

موجز علمي: راجع العلامات التي تهدد الإبصار فورًا. المجموعات المرضية الرئيسية: قرنية (عدوى/قرحة)، زاوية العين (ارتفاع الضغط الحاد)، الحيز المحجري المضغوط، ثغرة غلوبية، انسدادات للأوعية، والتهاب مناعى (مثل GCA).

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Key discriminators:
  - Dendritic fluorescein staining → HSV keratitis.
  - Contact‑lens user + severe pain / ring infiltrate or rapid worsening → Pseudomonas or Acanthamoeba keratitis.
  - Corneal ulcer ± hypopyon → bacterial keratitis (urgent).
  - Red painful eye + mid‑dilated non‑reactive pupil + cloudy cornea + N/V → acute angle‑closure glaucoma (AACG).
  - Sudden flashing lights/floaters then curtain → retinal detachment.
  - Sudden painless profound loss with pale retina/cherry‑red spot → CRAO (arterial occlusion).
  - Proptosis, tense lid, restricted EOM, painful swelling, raised IOP → OCS / orbital cellulitis / retrobulbar haemorrhage.
  - Periorbital swelling without EOM/vision involvement → preseptal cellulitis; with systemic features or EOM restriction → postseptal (orbital) cellulitis.
  - Localised sectoral redness that blanches with phenylephrine → episcleritis; non‑blanching, severe pain → scleritis.
- Red flags (act now): new ↓vision, severe or worsening pain, hypopyon, opaque cornea, fixed mid‑dilated pupil, RAPD, proptosis/tension, penetrating injury signs, chemical burn.

ملاحظات التعرف: افصل بين أسباب العين الحمراء بواسطة نقاط فحص أساسية: مظهر القرنية-الحجرة الأمامية-حدقة العين-حركة خارجية-بروتروز. أي علم يدل على تهديد الإبصار يحتاج إحالة فورية.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- Immediate bedside bundle: visual acuity (near & distance), pupils (APD/RAPD), extraocular movements, slit‑lamp / torch exam, fluorescein staining, seidel test if trauma suspected, IOP if globe intact (tonometry), external exam for proptosis.
- Imaging/lab triggers:
  - CT orbits non‑contrast for trauma with suspected fracture, intraorbital foreign body, retrobulbar haemorrhage, orbital cellulitis surgical planning.
  - B‑scan ultrasound when fundus view obscured (dense hyphema, corneal opacity, vitreous haemorrhage) to assess retinal detachment.
  - ESR/CRP and fast GCA pathway if temporal arteritis suspected with visual symptoms.
  - Microbiology: corneal scrapings for microbial keratitis; vitreous/aqueous cultures in endophthalmitis — do not delay empiric therapy for sampling if vision threatened.
- Interpretation tips:
  - Phenylephrine blanching differentiates episcleritis (blanches) from scleritis (does not).
  - Dendritic pattern under fluorescein = HSV; pseudodendrites (VZV) often raised and less staining.
  - Positive Seidel = globe leak/open globe → shield and urgent ophth; do not measure IOP.

النهج التشخيصي: فحوص أساسية عند المنضدة، تصوير أو قياسات محددة عند اشتباه بالتهاب محجرى أو عدم وضوح القاع. لا تؤخر العلاج في حالات تهدد الإبصار لأجل زرع/عينات فقط.

4. Emergency management algorithms
A. Triage algorithm (ED short pathway)
- If any red‑flag (see §2) → urgent ophthalmology contact + consider admission/transfer.
- If no red‑flag and simple conjunctivitis/allergic → outpatient tx and arranged follow‑up.

B. Orbital compartment syndrome (OCS) algorithm
- Recognise clinically (rapid proptosis, tense lids/orbit, severe pain, RAPD, decreased vision, high IOP). Do NOT wait for imaging.
- Immediate: lateral canthotomy ± inferior cantholysis at bedside.
  - Key steps: instil local anaesthetic (commonly 1 mL 1% lidocaine), crush lateral canthus ~1–2 min, cut lateral canthal tendon, divide inferior crus first; if no improvement divide superior crus; then urgent ophth. [Eyewiki; exam stepwise from Q900]
- After decompression: CT + definitive surgical/ophth care.

C. Acute angle‑closure glaucoma (AACG) algorithm
- Immediate: analgesia, antiemetic, lower IOP emergently and contact ophthalmology for definitive therapy (laser iridotomy).
- ED IOP‑lowering (common practice): IV acetazolamide 500 mg; topical beta‑blocker (timolol); topical alpha‑agonist; topical pilocarpine 2% may be given after some IOP reduction (timing varies in practice); consider IV mannitol if refractory. Admit/urgent ophth. [NICE CKS]
- Avoid treatments that increase inflammation or pressure inadvertently; verify sequence with local ophth.

D. Corneal ulcer/keratitis pathway
- Contact‑lens wearers or central/vision‑threatening ulcers: start intensive topical broad‑spectrum antibiotic drops immediately (e.g., topical fluoroquinolone per local protocol hourly/near‑hourly) and urgent ophthalmology. Do NOT patch. Avoid topical steroids until cleared by ophthalmology. Consider corneal scraping for culture if possible.

E. Endophthalmitis pathway
- Suspect after intraocular surgery/penetrating trauma with severe pain, ↓vision, hypopyon. Immediate ophthalmology; intravitreal antibiotics and vitrectomy decision per specialist. Obtain cultures if feasible.

F. Chemical eye injury
- Immediate copious irrigation (until pH neutral); measure surface pH after initial 5 min irrigation and repeat. Analgesia; topical anaesthetic short‑term ok to facilitate irrigation. Ophth consult urgently.

G. Hyphaema (traumatic anterior chamber blood)
- Shield eye, head elevation, bed rest, cycloplegic (e.g., cyclopentolate), topical steroid per ophthalmology, avoid NSAIDs/anticoagulants. Monitor IOP; urgent ophth if IOP >30 mmHg, total hyphaema, corneal blood staining, or rebleed. [Eyewiki]

H. Preseptal vs orbital cellulitis
- Preseptal (mild, no EOM restriction): oral antibiotics outpatient if reliable follow‑up.
- Orbital (EOM restriction/proptosis/vision loss/fever): IV broad‑spectrum antibiotics, urgent imaging, admit, ophthalmology/ENT consult.

خلاصة الخوارزميات: أي حالة مهددة رؤية → خوارزمية عاجلة مع اتصال بطبيب العيون، OCS يحتاج قِنطرة جانبية فورية، الزاوية الضيقة → خفض ضغط سريع ثم ليزر، قرحة قرنية لمرتدي العدسات → مضاد حيوي موضعي مكثف فوراً، إصابة كيميائية → ريّ فوري حتى التعادل.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
Drugs (ED‑focused, commonly used)
- Acetazolamide IV 500 mg (single dose commonly used in AACG) — verify local protocol. [NICE CKS]
- Pilocarpine 2% (topical) for AACG after partial IOP reduction; some use 4% for dark irides — practice varies.
- Topical broad‑spectrum fluoroquinolone drops for suspected bacterial keratitis (hourly or per protocol); avoid topical steroids until ophth review.
- Topical ganciclovir ointment for HSV dendritic keratitis (e.g., 5×/day for 7–10 days in common regimens).
- Analgesia/antiemetics as required; topical cycloplegic (cyclopentolate) for hyphaema pain control.
- IV methylprednisolone 500–1000 mg daily is used in many centres for GCA with visual symptoms — verify local guidance. (Flagged for verification.)

Procedures (ED)
- Lateral canthotomy + inferior cantholysis for OCS (perform without imaging if clinical).
- Seidel test (fluorescein) for globe leak.
- Removal of superficial corneal foreign body by skilled operator; rust ring removal arranged within 24–48 h by ophth.
- B‑scan ultrasound of globe when fundal view obscured.
- Do NOT patch suspected microbial keratitis, do NOT measure IOP if open globe suspected.

Disposition / follow‑up
- Urgent ophthalmology or transfer: any vision‑threatening sign, open globe, OCS, AACG, endophthalmitis, sight‑threatening keratitis, orbital cellulitis, large/unclearing hyphaema, suspected retinal detachment/CRAO.
- Admit: orbital cellulitis, vision‑threatening infections, uncontrolled IOP/hyphaema, post‑op or penetrating trauma high‑risk cases.
- Outpatient with timely follow‑up: mild preseptal cellulitis, simple conjunctivitis (if no red flags), chalazion after conservative measures.
- Safety net: advise return for any worsening pain, ↓vision, increasing redness, or inability to open eye.

توصيات العلاج والمتابعة بالعربية: جرعات ومبادئ عامة مدرجة أعلاه؛ إجراءات فورية تشمل قِنطرة جانبية للـ OCS، ريّ للبشرة الكيميائية، مضاد حيوي موضعى مكثف لعدوى القرنية لدى مرتدي العدسات. إحالة فورية لأمراض العيون عند أي علامة مهددة للإبصار.

6. High‑yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- Phenylephrine blanching test: episcleritis blanches; scleritis does not — classic exam trick.
- Dendritic fluorescein staining = HSV keratitis; pseudodendrites (VZV) are raised and stain less.
- Always ask about contact‑lens use — changes urgency and empiric therapy.
- Severe pain out of proportion to external findings → Acanthamoeba suspicion (contact‑lens history) or deep corneal involvement.
- Don’t delay lateral canthotomy for CT if OCS suspected — many questions cue tense orbit + high IOP → perform canthotomy immediately. (See Q900 technique.)
- In suspected open globe (misshapen pupil, flat AC, Seidel +) — shield, nil by mouth, tetanus, urgent transfer; DO NOT measure IOP or apply pressure.
- Avoid topical steroids in suspected microbial keratitis until reviewed by ophthalmology — common pitfall that worsens infection.
- CRAO acute maneuvers (ocular massage, paracentesis) are of limited evidence — mark as low‑evidence in exam answers; focus on urgent ophth and stroke pathway.
- Pilocarpine timing: may fail if given too late; many sources advise after partial IOP reduction — flag as exam caveat.

نصائح الامتحان بالعربية: اختبار فينيل يفرق الإبيسكليرا عن السكليرا؛ اسأل دائمًا عن العدسات؛ لا تؤخر قِنطرة جانبية عند المظاهر المميزة لـ OCS؛ تجنب الستيرويدات الموضعية في الشك بعدوى قرنية.

7. Arabic rapid‑review summary
- استرشد بعلامات التهديد: فقدان بصر مفاجئ، ألم شديد، هيبوباين/هيفيمة، قرحة قرنية، حدقة ثابتة/متوسّطة، ضغط عين مرتفع، بروتروز، RAPD.  
- OCS → قِنطرة جانبية + قطع وتر جفني فوري (لا تنتظر تصوير).  
- AACG → خفض ضغط العين فوراً (أسيتازولاميد IV 500 mg شائع) ثم إحالة عاجلة للـ iridotomy.  
- عدوى قرنية لدى مستخدمي العدسات → مضاد حيوي موضعي مكثف فوري؛ لا تضع رقعة، لا تعطي ستيرويد موضعي قبل مراجعة أخصائي.  
- إصابة كيميائية → ري فوري حتى تعادل الأس الهيدروجيني.  
- GCA بصرى → بدء ستيرويد نظامى فورى قبل الانتظار لتأكيد الاختبارات.  
- مفاتيح عملية: فحص البصر والحدقة، فلورسئين، Seidel، قياس ضغط العين إن كان آمناً، تصوير أو م‑الموجة عند الحاجة.

موجز بالعربية: ركّز على استبعاد الحالات المهددة للإبصار فورا، ابدأ التدخلات الأساسية المنقذة للحياة البصرية ثم إحالة متخصصة.

8. Question coverage map (sources / concepts covered)
- Q900 — lateral canthotomy / inferior cantholysis technique, indications (orbital compartment syndrome) [exam step reference].
- Corneal pathology (keratitis/ulcer/HSV/Acanthamoeba/Pseudomonas) — frequent FRCEM red‑eye questions.
- Acute angle‑closure glaucoma (recognition, ED IOP lowering, pilocarpine caveat).
- Orbital vs preseptal cellulitis; imaging and antibiotic escalation.
- Open globe / penetrating trauma (Seidel +, shielding, transfer).
- Chemical eye injury (irrigation, pH checks).
- Endophthalmitis (post‑op/penetrating trauma, intravitreal antibiotics).
- Hyphaema and traumatic anterior chamber blood (monitoring, IOP thresholds).
- Retinal vascular events (CRAO/CRVO) and retinal detachment (photopsia/floaters/curtain) — acute recognition and referral.
- Scleritis vs episcleritis (phenylephrine test; systemic associations).
- Giant cell arteritis with ocular ischemia (urgent steroids and pathways).
(Use your local question bank index to map above topics to specific Q‑ranges in your revision set.)

مصدر الربط للأسئلة: Q900 مذكور صراحة؛ بقية المواضيع مغطاة عبر بنوك أسئلة FRCEM القياسية حول العين الحمراء، الصدمة، والعدوى.

Sources to verify
- Eyewiki (topic pages cited) [Eyewiki]  
- NICE CKS — Acute glaucoma guidance [NICE CKS]  
- NCBI / Endophthalmitis reviews [NCBI]  
- Local ophthalmology protocols and formulary for exact topical antibiotic choice/dosing and pilocarpine sequencing  
- RCEM, ERC, NICE, AAO and local hospital ophthalmology guidelines for definitive recommendations and any dose/regimen variations

Final note: This chapter is high‑yield ED guidance for exam preparation. Verify local formularies and national guidelines for exact drug preparations, concentrations, and institutional procedures before clinical use.


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 16: NEUROLOGY
============================================================================================

Chapter 16 — Neurology (Compact FRCEM final chapter)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Framework: categorise emergencies by mechanism — vascular (ischaemia, SAH, CVST, dissection), haemorrhagic (SDH, EDH, SAH), infective/inflammatory (meningitis, encephalitis, epidural abscess), compressive (cord compression, cauda equina), seizure disorders/status epilepticus, neuromuscular (GBS, myasthenia gravis), neuro-ophthalmic (optic neuritis, CRAO/AION, HZO), primary headache syndromes (cluster, migraine, IIH), functional (NEAD).  
- Localisation rules (high-yield): cervical myotomes — C5 shoulder abduction; C6 elbow flexion & wrist extension; C7 elbow extension & wrist flexion; C8 finger flexors/thumb extension; T1 intrinsic hand. Dermatomes — C7 = middle finger; T10 = umbilicus. Lumbar LP landmark ≈ iliac crest ~ L4. Brown‑Séquard: ipsilateral motor/proprioception loss, contralateral pain/temp loss.  
- Pathophysiology short points:
  - SAH — aneurysmal rupture → blood in subarachnoid space; thunderclap headache; risk of vasospasm days 3–14.  
  - Subdural haematoma — torn bridging veins, gradual evolution (hyperdense → isodense → hypodense). Elderly/atrophy/anticoagulated predispose.  
  - CVST — venous outflow obstruction → raised ICP, venous infarct/haemorrhage; risk factors postpartum, OCP, thrombophilia, COVID.  
  - GBS — postinfectious immune neuropathy; ascending weakness, areflexia; respiratory decline risk.  
  - Spinal epidural abscess — local infection → cord compression; back pain + fever + neuro deficit classic triad (may present incompletely).

Arabic (مختصر): رتب الطوارئ العصبية حسب الآلية (وعائي، نزفي، التهابي، ضاغط، اعتلال عصبي). استخدم قواعد توطين العضلات والحسّ للتمييز الجذري/نخاعي. SAH = صداع رعدي؛ خراج فوق الجافية يسبب ألم ظهري وحمى وضعف عصبي.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Red flags (act urgently):
  - “Worst ever” thunderclap headache ± meningism → SAH (CT ± LP).  
  - Fever + neck stiffness ± photophobia ± reduced GCS → meningitis (start antibiotics immediately).  
  - New focal neuro deficit, progressive bilateral leg weakness, saddle anaesthesia, urinary retention → cauda equina (MRI lumbar urgently).  
  - Back pain + fever ± focal deficit → spinal epidural abscess (MRI spine).  
  - Seizure >5 min or repeated without recovery → status epilepticus (immediate BZD → second line).  
  - Rapidly progressive ascending weakness or FVC fall → Guillain‑Barré (monitor FVC, ICU threshold).  
  - New visual disturbance ± jaw claudication ± scalp tenderness in elderly → Giant Cell Arteritis (start steroids urgently).  
  - New unilateral painful visual loss with painful eye movements and colour desaturation in a young patient → optic neuritis (MS association).  
  - Facial palsy with ear vesicles → Ramsay Hunt (antiviral + steroid). Forehead involvement: peripheral; forehead sparing: central.  
  - Posterior neck/head pain after trauma/manipulation with brainstem/cerebellar signs → vertebral artery dissection.

- Key differentials by presentation:
  - Thunderclap headache: SAH vs reversible cerebral vasoconstriction vs migraine/other — use CT ± LP/CTA.  
  - Acute painless monocular vision loss → CRAO/AION; painful with movement → optic neuritis.  
  - Acute bilateral headache + papilloedema + normal neuroimaging → IIH (LP opening pressure high).  
  - New focal seizures or focal status → structural lesion/abscess/CVA; perform imaging.

Arabic (مختصر): اشارات حمراء: صداع رعدي، حمى مع تيبس رقبة، ضعف تصاعدي مع انخفاض FVC، ألم ظهري مع حمى. شلل وجهي كامل = محيطي؛ حفظ الجبين = مركزي.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- General ED priorities: ABCs, oxygen, IV access, blood glucose, blood cultures if infection suspected, baseline bloods (FBC, U&Es, coagulation), ECG/oximetry. Consider drug/toxicology and anticonvulsant levels when relevant.  
- Imaging & procedure algorithms (concise):
  - Suspected bacterial meningitis:
    1) Blood cultures → give IV ceftriaxone 2 g immediately. Add IV amoxicillin if Listeria risk (age >50, immunocompromised) [NICE NG240 2024].  
    2) If focal neurology, papilloedema, reduced GCS, or seizure → CT head before LP. If LP delayed, do not delay antibiotics/dexamethasone where indicated.  
    3) Interpret CSF: neutrophils ↓glucose ↑protein = bacterial; lymphocytes normal glucose = viral; xanthochromia = SAH if present after 12+ hours.
  - Suspected SAH (thunderclap):
    1) Non‑contrast CT head immediately. If high‑quality CT within 6 h and negative (interpreted appropriately) sensitivity is high [NICE NG228]; if CT negative but clinical suspicion remains → LP for xanthochromia (best ≥12 h after onset).  
    2) If CT positive → CTA/angiography to locate aneurysm; neurosurgical/neuroradiology referral.  
  - Suspected stroke/dissection/posterior circulation:
    - Non‑contrast CT head ± CTA head/neck for dissection; MRI if available for posterior fossa.
  - Suspected CVST: CT venogram (CTV) or MR venogram (MRV). Start anticoagulation promptly once diagnosis suspected/confirmed.
  - Suspected spinal epidural abscess / cauda equina / cord compression: urgent MRI spine with contrast; do not wait for plain films.  
  - Status epilepticus: glucose ± point-of-care electrolytes immediately; urgent CT head if focal onset or prolonged/protracted events.
- Specific test interpretation tips:
  - CT timing matters for SAH sensitivity — note time from headache onset.  
  - CSF opening pressure: >25 cm H2O supports IIH (confirm units locally).  
  - Distinguish central vs peripheral facial palsy by forehead involvement.

Arabic (مختصر): فحص سريع: ثقافات دم، Ceftriaxone 2 غ للالتهاب السحائي، CT قبل LP إذا علامات ارتفاع ضغط أو بؤرية. صداع رعدي → CT فورًا؛ CT سلبي لكن اشتباه مستمر → بزل قطني بعد 12 ساعة للزانثوكروميا. MRI للعمود عند اشتباه خراج أو ذيل الفرس؛ CTV/MRV لـCVST.

4. Emergency management algorithms
- Meningitis (adult suspected bacterial):
  - Algorithm: ABC → blood cultures → IV ceftriaxone 2 g immediately; add IV amoxicillin if Listeria risk; give dexamethasone where pneumococcal meningitis suspected and per local guidance (before/with first antibiotic) [NICE NG240 2024]. Do CT head before LP if any contraindication.  
- SAH (acute thunderclap with CT positive):
  - Immediate: admit/observation in high-dependency, nimodipine 60 mg orally q4h as soon as SAH diagnosed (start pre-transfer), control systolic BP to local target (common target <160 mmHg but follow local protocol), arrange urgent neurosurgical/neuroradiology definitive management (coiling/clipping), give seizure prophylaxis if indicated.  
- Status epilepticus (adult):
  - Step 1 (0–5 min): ABC, oxygen, IV access, check glucose; give benzodiazepine IV (lorazepam 4 mg IV bolus; repeat once if needed) — if no IV available use IN/IM/buccal midazolam (IN 0.2 mg/kg, max 10 mg) or rectal diazepam.  
  - Step 2 (5–20 min persistent): Give second‑line IV antiseizure drug per local protocol — levetiracetam load commonly used (e.g., 60 mg/kg reported in sources; many centres use 1–2 g IV) or fosphenytoin/phenytoin/valproate depending on contraindications. (Verify local loading dose caps.)  
  - Step 3 (>30 min refractory): Prepare airway, request ICU, induce anaesthesia (propofol/thiopental/ketamine per protocol), continuous EEG if available.  
- Spinal epidural abscess / cord compression / cauda equina:
  - Urgent MRI spine → start empiric broad-spectrum IV antibiotics covering S. aureus (include MRSA per risk) → urgent neurosurgical review for decompression if cord compromise or progressive deficits; keep NPO and monitor neuro/urinary function.  
- CVST:
  - Start therapeutic anticoagulation (LMWH) promptly once suspected/confirmed even with haemorrhagic transformation (evidence supports treating thrombosis); arrange MRV/CTV and neurology/hematology input. Evolving recommendations on DOACs — verify local guidance.  
- Guillain‑Barré syndrome:
  - Monitor serial FVC and bedside strength; ICU/ventilation if rapid decline or FVC thresholds approached (common trigger values 15–20 mL/kg); start IVIG or plasmapheresis early once diagnosis made.  
- GCA with visual symptoms:
  - Start high‑dose corticosteroids urgently to reduce risk of permanent visual loss — route/dose regimens vary; confirm local protocol (many centres use high‑dose IV/PO steroids immediately). Obtain ESR/CRP and urgent specialist review.  
- Wernicke’s encephalopathy:
  - Give high‑dose parenteral thiamine immediately BEFORE glucose; follow local Pabrinex/BNF protocol.  
- IIH:
  - Neuroimaging (MRI/MRV) to exclude mass/venous sinus thrombosis; if normal and criteria met perform LP for opening pressure; treat with acetazolamide/weight loss and urgent ophthalmology if vision threatened.

Arabic (مختصر): خوارزميات سريعة — التهاب سحائي: ثقافات ثم Ceftriaxone 2 غ فورًا ± Amoxicillin عند خطر Listeria. SAH: نيموديبين 60 ملغ كل 4 ساعات و نقل جراحي عاجل. نوبة مستمرة: بنزو أولاً (لورازيبام 4 ملغ) → حمولة مضاد صرع IV → تخدير/ICU إذا مقاومة. خراج فوق الجافية: MRI عاجل، مضادات حيوية جدارية، جراحة.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
- Key drugs & well‑established doses:
  - Ceftriaxone adult meningitis empiric: 2 g IV immediately after cultures [NICE NG240 2024].  
  - Nimodipine for aneurysmal SAH: 60 mg orally q4h (start ASAP) [BNF/national practice].  
  - Benzodiazepines in status: lorazepam 4 mg IV (may repeat once); if no IV available use intranasal midazolam 0.2 mg/kg (max 10 mg).  
  - Second‑line anti‑seizure: levetiracetam commonly used as IV load (many protocols 1–2 g; some sources state 60 mg/kg — verify local caps). Phenytoin/fosphenytoin or valproate are alternatives per local guidance. (Verify exact institutional doses.)  
  - IV acyclovir for encephalitis/HZO in immunocompromised or sight‑threatening disease: 10 mg/kg IV q8h (verify local formulary).  
  - Prednisolone for Bell’s palsy: typical exam regimens ~50 mg PO daily for 7–10 days if within 72 h (local regimens vary).  
  - GBS: IVIG or plasmapheresis per neurology guidance (dose regimens per protocol).  
- Procedures and key practical points:
  - Lumbar puncture: check indications/contraindications (TAP AS IF mnemonic: Trauma, Age >60, Papilloedema, Altered mental status, Seizure, Immunocompromise, Focal deficit); use lateral decubitus for opening pressure measurement; use pencil‑point needles to reduce PDPH when possible.  
  - Imaging: CT/CTA/CTV/MRI/MRV — choose modality targeted to pathology (CT first for bleed; MRI spine for cord/SEA; CTV/MRV for CVST).  
  - Airway management: anticipate early in refractory SE, GBS with falling FVC, myasthenic crisis. Document baseline FVC and bulbar function.  
  - Anticoagulation reversal: for intracranial haemorrhage on anticoagulants, follow local haematology/anticoagulation reversal protocols; reverse anticoagulation urgently for expanding SDH with neurosurgical input.  
- Disposition & follow‑up (general):
  - Immediate neurosurgical/neuroradiology transfer: SAH with bleed or mass effect SDH/EDH, posterior fossa stroke with mass effect, spinal cord compression with progressive deficit.  
  - Admit HDU/ICU: status epilepticus not terminated, airway/ventilatory compromise, severe GBS, unstable SAH, septic/unstable meningitis.  
  - Urgent specialty review: ophthalmology for optic neuritis/HZO with ocular involvement, neurology for suspected GBS/CVST/encephalitis, ENT/ophthalmology for Ramsay Hunt/HZO, rheumatology/ophthalmology for GCA.  
  - Outpatient follow‑up: IIH weight management and neurology/ophthalmology follow-up; Bell’s palsy follow‑up for incomplete recovery; post‑SAH vascular clinic and neuropsychological support.

Arabic (مختصر): أدوية ومداخلات مهمة: Ceftriaxone 2 غ للالتهاب السحائي، Nimodipine 60 ملغ/4س للـSAH، لورازيبام 4 ملغ IV للنوبة المستمرة. إجراءات: بزل قطني مع الانتباه لموانع (TAP AS IF)، MRI للحبل عند اشتباه خُراج أو ذيل الفرس. إحالة عاجلة لجراحة/ICU حسب الاستقرار والنتائج.

6. High‑yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- Localisation mnemonics and exam tricks:
  - Key myotome checks: C6 = elbow flexion/wrist extension; C7 = elbow extension/wrist flexion; test middle finger sensation for C7. Dermatomes: T10 = umbilicus. Nipple = T4. L4 = iliac crest landmark for LP. [LITFL referenced]  
  - Facial palsy: forehead involvement → peripheral (Bell’s/Ramsay Hunt); forehead sparing → central (UMN) — classic exam discriminator.  
  - Diplopia: if cover of either eye abolishes diplopia → binocular (neuro). If diplopia persists with cover of one eye → monocular (ocular).  
  - Seizure vs NEAD: resistance to eye opening during event, asynchronous limb movements, long duration without post‑ictal state → suggests NEAD.  
- Common exam pitfalls:
  - Don’t assume a negative CT rules out SAH if scan is delayed or image quality/documentation suboptimal; recall CT sensitivity falls with time — LP/CTA may still be required [NICE NG228 caveats].  
  - In suspected bacterial meningitis do not delay empiric antibiotics for LP/CT if obtaining imaging would delay treatment. Use TAP AS IF to decide CT before LP.  
  - In CVST do not withhold anticoagulation solely because of haemorrhagic parenchymal lesions — treat thrombosis after specialist input; check up‑to‑date guidance on DOACs.  
  - For status epilepticus, many exam stems test repeating benzodiazepine once before escalating — do not skip this. Prepare airway early.  
  - Be cautious with dose statements that vary by institution: levetiracetam 60 mg/kg loading and IV methylprednisolone 1 g/day for GCA are used in some centres — verify local guidelines before stating as universal.  
  - Cauda equina red flags: saddle anaesthesia, urinary retention, severe/progressive bilateral weakness; age 16–50 alone is not a red flag (NICE/CKS).  
  - Do not give aminoglycosides to myasthenic patients; watch drug lists that exacerbate neuromuscular weakness.  
- Exam memory aids:
  - TAP AS IF = reasons to do CT before LP (Trauma, Age>60, Papilloedema, Altered mental status, Seizure, Immunocompromise, Focal deficit).  
  - “Worst headache → CT; CT negative and high suspicion → LP for xanthochromia (≥12 h optimal).”

Arabic (مختصر): حيل الامتحان: فَرِّق بين شلل وجه محيطي/مركزي بواسطة الجبين؛ اختبار تغطية العين يميّز ازدواج أحادي/ثنائي؛ TAP AS IF لمعايير CT قبل LP. أخطاء شائعة: افتراض استبعاد SAH بواسطة CT متأخر؛ تأخير المضادّات في التهاب السحايا عند الحاجة.

7. Arabic rapid-review summary (مراجعة سريعة بالعربية الفصحى)
- قِسِّم الطوارئ العصبية حسب الآلية: وعائي، نزفي، التهابي، ضغطى، عصبي طرفي، أداء وظيفي.  
- إشارات حمراء: صداع رعدي، حمى مع تيبس رقبى، ضعف تصاعدي مع تناقص FVC، ألم ظهري مع حمى أو ضعف عصبي، فقدان بؤري مفاجئ للرؤية، شلل وجهي جديد.  
- إجراءات عاجلة: Ceftriaxone 2 غ IV فورًا عند اشتباه التهاب سحائي؛ CT فورًا عند صداع رعدي؛ لورازيبام 4 ملغ IV لنوبة مستمرة (كرر مرة) ثم حمولة مضاد صرع IV؛ MRI عند اشتباه خراج فوق الجافية أو ذيل الفرس؛ ابدأ علاج تجريبي إذا تأخرت الفحوص.  
- نقاط عملية: راقب FVC في GBS، لا تؤخر المضادّات الحيوية من أجل صور/LP إذا التأخير قد يضر، ابدأ الستيرويد عالي الجرعة فورًا عند اشتباه GCA بأعراض بصرية، أجرِ LP لقياس ضغط الفتح إذا IIH مشتبه.  
- تحقق محليًا من جرعات ليفتيراسيتام التحميلية ونماذج جرعات الستيرويد وجوانب استخدام DOACs في CVST.

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- Meningitis/encephalitis — empiric antibiotics, CT-before-LP decision making, dexamethasone [NICE NG240 2024].  
- Subarachnoid haemorrhage — thunderclap headache, CT/LP/CTA pathway, nimodipine and neurosurgical transfer [NICE NG228, BNF].  
- Intracranial haemorrhage patterns — SDH/EDH evolutions and anticoagulation reversal.  
- Status epilepticus — benzodiazepine first line, second-line antiseizure options, refractory management (ICU).  
- Seizure vs NEAD differentiation (eye‑opening resistance etc.).  
- Spinal emergencies — cauda equina, spinal epidural abscess, cord compression (MRI urgent, antibiotics, decompression).  
- Guillain‑Barré syndrome — ascending weakness, FVC monitoring, IVIG/plasmapheresis.  
- Cerebral venous sinus thrombosis — presentation, CTV/MRV, anticoagulation (LMWH), DOACs evolution.  
- Cervical artery dissection/vertebral dissection — posterior neck pain, posterior circulation stroke, CTA.  
- Neuro‑ophthalmology — optic neuritis, CRAO/AION, HZO/Ramsay Hunt, Hutchinson sign.  
- Headache emergencies and primary headaches — cluster headache oxygen/sumatriptan, medication‑overuse headache, IIH diagnosis (LP opening pressure).  
- Peripheral facial palsy — Bell’s palsy vs Ramsay Hunt; forehead involvement sign.  
- Myasthenia gravis & myasthenic crisis — drug precipitants, thymoma screening.  
- Wernicke’s encephalopathy — give thiamine before glucose (Pabrinex references).  
- ENT/ophthalmology intersections — HZO vision threats (IV acyclovir).  
- Practical procedural items — LP technique/contraindications, needle choices, landmark L4/iliac crest.

Sources to verify
- NICE guidance NG240 (meningitis) 2024; NICE NG228 (SAH/CT timing) and CKS summaries; BNF; RCEM and ERC acute neurology/stroke/seizure guidance; LITFL (myotomes/localisation); CADISS trial (2015) for cervical artery dissection context; local hospital formularies and neurology/haematology policies for dosing, anticoagulation and steroid regimens.

(Study note: this chapter is high‑yield summary material for exam preparation — verify local treatment protocols and up‑to‑date national guidance before clinical application.)


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 17: OBSTETRICS & GYNAECOLOGY
============================================================================================

Chapter 17 — Obstetrics & Gynaecology (final)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Priorities: maternal ABCs first; once mother stabilised, assess foetus (gestational age, CTG/USS). Many management choices depend on gestation, maternal haemodynamic status and placental/cord pathology.
- Pregnancy physiology alters baseline values and investigation interpretation (raised baseline D‑dimer, increased blood volume, altered pharmacokinetics). Some drugs/imaging avoided or modified in pregnancy.
- Common immediate obstetric emergencies:
  - Early pregnancy: ectopic pregnancy, miscarriage.
  - Late pregnancy/third trimester: placenta praevia (painless PV bleeding), placental abruption (painful bleeding ± uterine tenderness), cord prolapse (acute foetal compromise), uterine rupture (prior scar + acute abdominal catastrophe), severe pre‑eclampsia/HELLP, veno‑thromboembolism, sepsis/TSS, postpartum haemorrhage (PPH).
- Key pathophysiology notes:
  - Ectopic: implantation outside uterus → risk of tubal rupture and intra‑abdominal haemorrhage.
  - Abruption: premature placental separation → maternal haemorrhage, uterine tetany, foetal hypoxia.
  - Placenta praevia: low‑lying/covering os → painless major bleeding; digital vaginal exam contraindicated until excluded by ultrasound.
  - HELLP: microangiopathic haemolysis + elevated liver enzymes + low platelets → rapid deterioration; delivery is definitive treatment.
  - PPH: uterine atony most common; retained products, trauma, coagulopathy also causes.

Arabic summary:
الأولوية إنعاش الأم ثم تقييم الجنين. الحمل يغير القيم التشخيصية والعلاجية. انتبه للحمل خارج الرحم، انفصال المشيمة، المشيمة النازحة، تسلل الحبل، تمزق الرحم، HELLP، ونزف ما بعد الولادة.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Vaginal bleeding:
  - First trimester: threatened miscarriage vs ectopic — pain, haemodynamic instability, adnexal tenderness/mass suggest ectopic.
  - Second/third trimester: painless PV bleeding → placenta praevia until excluded; painful bleeding + uterine tenderness → abruption; sudden severe pain + loss of foetal heart sounds in scarred uterus → uterine rupture.
- Abdominal pain + positive pregnancy test: localised unilateral pain or adnexal mass → ectopic until proven otherwise.
- Reduced/absent foetal movements after 24 wks → urgent CTG (≥30 min) and consider bedside USS.
- Hypertension after 20 wks:
  - New BP ≥140/90: pre‑eclampsia.
  - Severe features: BP ≥160/110 (two readings ≥4 h apart if time allows), severe headache, visual disturbance, epigastric/RUQ pain, oliguria, thrombocytopenia.
- HELLP red flags: RUQ/epigastric pain, nausea/vomiting, AST/ALT markedly raised, platelets <100×10^9/L, haemolysis (raised LDH).
- Shock + high fever + rash in menstruating tampon user → toxic shock syndrome (TSS).
- VTE: unilateral calf pain/swelling → DVT; dyspnoea/pleuritic pain/tachycardia → consider PE.
- Postpartum fever ± uterine tenderness → endometritis; mastitis presents with focal breast erythema/tenderness ± systemic illness.

Arabic summary:
نزف بلا ألم في الثلث الثالث يوجب استبعاد المشيمة النازحة. ألم مع نزف وحنان رحمى يوجّه للانفصال المشيمى. ضغط الدم ≥160/110 وعلامات عضوية يشيان بتسمم الحمل الشديد. توقف حركة الجنين بعد 24 أسبوعًا — راقب CTG فوراً.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- General:
  - If unstable: treat first; do not delay definitive intervention for imaging.
  - Labs: FBC, Group & Save/crossmatch, coagulation, LFTs, creatinine, urinalysis/urine protein, quantitative β‑hCG (early pregnancy), cultures if infection suspected.
- Early pregnancy bleeding:
  - TVUS first‑line to locate pregnancy; look for intrauterine gestational sac, yolk sac, free fluid.
  - Serial quantitative β‑hCG: exam rule — viable intrauterine pregnancy generally rises ≈≥66% in 48 h (approximate). Slow rise/plateau suggests ectopic/non‑viable pregnancy.
- Suspected ectopic and unstable → immediate surgical management.
- DVT/PE in pregnancy:
  - D‑dimer is often raised in pregnancy — limited utility alone.
  - DVT: compression duplex ultrasound of symptomatic leg(s) is first imaging.
  - PE imaging algorithm: obtain CXR first.
    - If CXR normal → V/Q scan often preferred (lower maternal breast dose).
    - If CXR abnormal → CTPA preferred.
  - Start therapeutic LMWH when clinical probability high and imaging delayed/unavailable (unless contraindicated).
- Reduced foetal movements:
  - ≥24 wks: CTG monitoring for ≥30 min.
  - <24 wks: bedside USS (foetal heart activity).
- Infection exposures:
  - Varicella exposure: immediate maternal VZV IgG serology; if non‑immune and within window consider VZIG per local policy.
  - Parvovirus B19: maternal IgM/IgG; if seroconversion → serial fetal USS for hydrops.
  - Genital HSV: primary maternal lesions near term → caesarean consideration; consult obstetrics.
- Trauma ≥22 wks: CTG monitoring (minimum 1 h), abdominal USS for placenta/abruption; check Rh status and consider Kleihauer to quantify FMH.

Arabic summary:
المريض غير المستقر يعالج فورًا. نزف الحمل المبكر يُقيّم بالموجات عبر المهبل وهرمون الحمل التكرارى. D‑dimer محدود الفائدة فى الحمل؛ الاشتباه بالصمة الرئوية يوجّه إلى CXR ثم V/Q أو CTPA تبعًا للنتيجة.

4. Emergency management algorithms
- Algorithm A — Haemodynamically unstable pregnant patient with bleeding (any gestation)
  1. ABCs, high‑flow O2, two large‑bore IVs, call senior obstetrics and anaesthetics/theatre.
  2. Send labs: FBC, coag, G&S ± crossmatch, LFTs, creatinine, β‑hCG (if early).
  3. Resuscitate: IV crystalloids judiciously, transfuse PRBC/FFP/platelets per massive haemorrhage protocol; give tranexamic acid 1 g IV early (within 3 h) for PPH if indicated.
  4. Specific: first‑trimester ongoing heavy bleeding → surgical evacuation; postpartum → uterotonics (oxytocin + adjuncts), bimanual massage, escalate to theatre if refractory.
- Algorithm B — Suspected ectopic (stable)
  1. TVUS ± serial β‑hCG.
  2. If ectopic on USS or high clinical suspicion with abnormal hCG trend → urgent gynae consult: methotrexate (medical) if criteria met OR surgical management (laparoscopy/laparotomy).
  3. If unstable → immediate transfer to theatre.
- Algorithm C — Cord prolapse with foetal compromise
  1. Immediate: position mother head‑down (Trendelenburg/left lateral or knee–chest), manual elevation of presenting part, avoid cord handling that compromises flow.
  2. Call theatre for immediate delivery (usually emergent Caesarean).
  3. Temporise: fill bladder or use tocolysis per local protocol if needed while preparing theatre.
- Algorithm D — Severe pre‑eclampsia / HELLP
  1. Stabilise: oxygen, IV access, labs, continuous fetal monitoring.
  2. Seizure prophylaxis: start MgSO4 (see section 5).
  3. BP control: give parenteral antihypertensive per local protocol; arrange urgent obstetric delivery.
  4. HELLP: prepare for expedited delivery; correct coagulopathy, transfuse platelets if indicated.
- Algorithm E — Suspected PE in pregnancy
  1. Assess stability. If unstable → resuscitate and consider immediate reperfusion per local guidance (consult specialists).
  2. Obtain CXR; choose V/Q if CXR normal, CTPA if CXR abnormal or V/Q unavailable.
  3. If high clinical suspicion and imaging delayed → start therapeutic LMWH.
- Algorithm F — Suspected maternal sepsis/TSS
  1. ABC + sepsis resuscitation bundle: fluids, cultures, empiric antibiotics.
  2. For suspected staphylococcal TSS: empiric vancomycin + clindamycin (clindamycin to inhibit toxin production); surgical source control if required.
- Algorithm G — VTE in pregnancy
  1. Confirm DVT with compression ultrasound.
  2. Treat confirmed DVT/PE with therapeutic LMWH throughout pregnancy (dose adjustments by weight and renal function).
  3. DOACs = not recommended in pregnancy.

Arabic summary:
خوارزميات الطوارئ: نزف حاد → إنعاش، نداء الفريق، نقل دم، وإجراءات جراحية مبكرة حسب السبب؛ اشتباه بالمشيمة النازحة → لا تفحص مهبليًا قبل السونار؛ الشك بصدمة سمية → فانكوميسين + كليندامايسين.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
- Key well‑established thresholds/doses (verify local protocols):
  - Severe pre‑eclampsia BP threshold: systolic ≥160 mmHg or diastolic ≥110 mmHg (two readings ≥4 h apart if time allows) → classed as severe disease.
  - MgSO4 for seizure prophylaxis in severe pre‑eclampsia: loading 4 g IV over 15–20 min, then maintenance infusion commonly 1 g/hr (monitor respirations, reflexes, urine output). (Follow local protocol.)
  - Tranexamic acid for PPH: 1 g IV as early as possible within 3 hours (then per protocol).
  - PPH definitions: minor 500–1000 mL; major ≥1000 mL in 24 h.
  - Anti‑D immunoglobulin: give after sensitising event within 72 hours; do not delay first dose while awaiting Kleihauer; quantify FMH (Kleihauer) to decide on additional dosing per local guideline.
  - LMWH: first‑line anticoagulation in pregnancy (therapeutic dosing for DVT/PE). DOACs not recommended.
  - TSS empiric antibiotics: vancomycin + clindamycin initially (change to anti‑staphylococcal β‑lactam if MSSA proven).
- Procedures:
  - TVUS for early pregnancy assessment and placenta localization.
  - CTG for foetal monitoring ≥24–26 wks (or local viability threshold); continuous monitoring if maternal/foetal concern.
  - Surgical: laparoscopy/laparotomy for ruptured ectopic or uterine rupture; urgent Caesarean for cord prolapse with foetal compromise or other obstetric emergencies.
  - Kleihauer–Betke or equivalent assay for FMH quantification in Rh‑negative mothers after bleeding/trauma.
- Disposition and follow‑up:
  - Unstable patients → resuscitate + transfer to labour ward/theatre/ICU as appropriate; consultant obstetric/anaesthetic involvement early.
  - Stable with ectopic → admit or organise urgent gynae follow‑up for methotrexate or elective surgery.
  - Confirmed DVT/PE → admit and continue therapeutic LMWH; arrange haematology/obstetric follow‑up and postpartum thromboprophylaxis plan.
  - PPH recovered → observe on labour ward, arrange haemoglobin check, and follow‑up for anaemia and lactation support.
  - Exposures (varicella, parvovirus, HIV/HSV) → document serology, liaise with obstetrics/ID, arrange neonatal planning and counselling.

Arabic summary:
نقاط علاجية: سلفات المغنيسيوم 4 جم تحميل ثم صيانة 1 غ/ساعة (تحقق من البروتوكول المحلى). ترانكساميك أسيد 1 غ IV مبكرًا فى PPH. مضاد‑D خلال 72 ساعة بعد حدث حسّاس—لا تؤخر الجرعة الأولية. LMWH للعلاج الوقائى والعلاجى للجلطات أثناء الحمل؛ DOACs غير مستحبة.

6. High‑yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- hCG dynamics: rise <66% in 48 h suggests failing/ectopic pregnancy; fall suggests miscarriage — commonly tested.
- Never perform digital/vaginal examination for bleeding after 24 weeks until placenta praevia is excluded by ultrasound — classic pitfall question.
- D‑dimer is frequently elevated during pregnancy—don’t use a single positive D‑dimer to rule in VTE; rely on imaging (compression US/CTPA/V‑Q).
- Imaging for suspected PE: start with CXR. If normal → V/Q scan often preferred; if abnormal → CTPA. This CXR‑guided algorithm is a frequent exam stem.
- In cord prolapse with persistent severe variable decelerations, immediate manual elevation/positioning and transfer for emergency delivery are exam focal points — do not wait for imaging.
- TSS: addition of clindamycin is to inhibit toxin production — an often‑tested justification for combination therapy (vancomycin + clindamycin).
- Anti‑D: do not delay giving initial anti‑D for a potentially sensitising event pending Kleihauer results — exam-style trap.
- VTE questions in pregnancy: LMWH is usually the correct answer; DOACs are common distractors.
- Methotrexate for ectopic: eligibility criteria (hCG cutoffs, sac size) vary — expect “contraindications include haemodynamic instability, suspected rupture, high hCG or significant hepatic/renal disease”.
- Hyperemesis: doxylamine + pyridoxine first-line oral therapy in many guidelines; ondansetron is effective but may appear as a distractor due to teratogenicity debates.

Arabic summary:
خدع الامتحان: ارتفاع هرمون الحمل أقل من 66% خلال 48 ساعة يثير الحمل خارج الرحم؛ لا تفحص المهبل بنزف بعد 24 أسبوعًا قبل السونار؛ D‑dimer قد يكون مرتفعًا فلا يعتمد عليه وحده؛ CXR يحدد اختيار V/Q أو CTPA.

7. Arabic rapid‑review summary (concise)
- أولوية إنقاذ الأم ثم تقييم الجنين.  
- النزف فى الثلث الثالث بلا ألم → استبعد المشيمة النازحة قبل الفحص المهبلي.  
- حمل خارج الرحم: ألم بطني أحادي الجانب + كتلة مبيضية محتملة + هرمون حمل يرتفع ببطء.  
- صدمة أو نزف حاد → إنعاش ABC، عزل وريدي واسع، فصيلة وG&S، مناداة فريق التوليد.  
- PPH كبير ≥1000 مل: مساج، أوكسي توسين/أدوية منظفة الرحم، ترانكساميك أسيد 1 غ IV مبكرا، ومنظومة نقل دم.  
- تسمم الحمل الشديد/HELLP → MgSO4 للوقاية من التشنجات (4 غ تحميل) + ضبط الضغط والتحضير للولادة.  
- جلطة وريدية/صمة: استخدم LMWH؛ تجنب DOACs أثناء الحمل.  
- التعرض لجدري الماء: فحص IgG وتعامل بمضاد‑فيروس أو VZIG حسب الحساسية والبروتوكول.  
- لا تؤخر جرعات مضاد‑D الأولية بعد حدث حساس; استخدم اختبار Kleihauer لتحديد جرعات إضافية.

8. Question coverage map (source Q‑number ranges / concepts covered)
- Q1–Q4: Early pregnancy bleeding, ectopic pregnancy diagnosis and management (hCG dynamics, TVUS, methotrexate vs surgery).  
- Q5–Q8: Placenta praevia vs placental abruption; vaginal bleeding in 2nd/3rd trimester; uterine rupture recognition.  
- Q9–Q11: Cord prolapse and immediate manoeuvres; foetal distress management and urgent delivery decision.  
- Q12–Q15: Pre‑eclampsia and HELLP (diagnostic thresholds, MgSO4, BP control, delivery indications).  
- Q16–Q18: Postpartum haemorrhage (definitions, uterotonics, tranexamic acid, MHP activation).  
- Q19–Q22: VTE in pregnancy (DVT/PE diagnostics, D‑dimer caveats, V/Q vs CTPA algorithm, LMWH therapy).  
- Q23–Q25: Maternal infections and exposures (varicella/VZIG, parvovirus B19, herpes/HIV neonatal considerations).  
- Q26–Q28: Maternal sepsis and TSS (empiric antibiotics including clindamycin rationale, source control).  
- Q29–Q31: Trauma in pregnancy (CTG ≥22 wks, FMH/Kleihauer, anti‑D timing), and obstetric imaging decisions.  
- Q32–Q34: Medications/procedures (MgSO4 dosing, tranexamic acid, anti‑D timing, LMWH contraindications).  
- Q35–Q37: Breast infections, mastitis vs abscess management, breastfeeding advice in HIV-positive mothers.  
- Q38–Q40: Exam pitfalls and common MCQ traps (D‑dimer, vaginal exam in 3rd trimester bleeding, imaging choices).

Arabic summary of coverage map:
خريطة الأسئلة تغطى: نزف الحمل المبكر والحمل خارج الرحم؛ النزف فى الثلث الثالث؛ تدبير اختناق الحبل والولادة الطارئة؛ تسمم الحمل/HELLP؛ نزف ما بعد الولادة؛ الجلطات الوريدية والصمة الرئوية؛ العدوى المتخصصة (جدري الماء، بارفو، هربس)؛ التسمم السمي/TSS؛ إصابات الحوامل؛ الأدوية والإجراءات الحيوية.

Sources to verify
- Royal College of Obstetricians & Gynaecologists (RCOG) guidance; Royal College of Emergency Medicine (RCEM) clinical resources; National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guidance; British National Formulary (BNF); local obstetric/hospital guidelines and national obstetric infectious disease guidance.


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 18: ONCOLOGICAL EMERGENCIES
============================================================================================

Chapter 18 — Oncological emergencies (compact FRCEM final chapter)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Definition: time-critical consequences of cancer or its treatment causing rapid organ compromise — common syndromes: metastatic spinal cord compression (MSCC), superior vena cava obstruction (SVCO), neutropenic (febrile) sepsis, tumour lysis syndrome (TLS), chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting (CINV) and treatment-related complications.
- Pathophysiology (core ideas):
  - MSCC: extradural tumour (metastasis or local extension) or vertebral collapse → cord/cauda compression → vascular congestion and ischaemia → progressive neurological loss.
  - SVCO: malignant mediastinal mass compresses SVC → impaired venous return from head/neck/upper chest → raised venous pressures, oedema, airway compromise risk.
  - Neutropenic sepsis: chemotherapy-induced marrow suppression → low neutrophils → impaired infection control; small infections can cause fulminant sepsis.
  - TLS: massive tumour cell lysis (spontaneous or after therapy) → release of K+, phosphate, nucleic acids → hyperkalaemia, hyperphosphataemia, hyperuricaemia → secondary hypocalcaemia and AKI.
  - CINV: drug-specific CNS/enteric receptor activation (5-HT3, NK1, dopamine) → acute/delayed/anticipatory patterns.
Arabic summary: الطوارئ الأورامية هي مضاعفات سريعة ومهددة للحياة لورم أو علاجه؛ آلياتها الرئيسية: ضغط الحبل الشوكي، انسداد الوريد الأجوف العلوي، تعفن مع قلة العدلات، تحلل سريع للخلايا الورمية واضطرابات القيء بعد العلاج.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- MSCC — red flags:
  - Progressive local back pain (worse supine/night), new focal motor weakness, sensory level, gait disturbance, urinary retention/incontinence or constipation.
  - Differentials: degenerative spine disease, vertebral fracture, epidural abscess (fever, ESR/CRP), primary spine tumour.
- SVCO — recognition:
  - Facial/neck/upper chest swelling, venous distension, dyspnoea/orthopnoea, cough, hoarseness, stridor/airway compromise.
  - Differentials: SVC thrombosis, heart failure, mediastinitis.
- Neutropenic (febrile) sepsis — red flags:
  - Any fever in recent chemotherapy patient → assume neutropenic sepsis until proven otherwise. Watch for hypotension, tachycardia, hypoxia, altered mental status.
  - Differentials: drug fever, tumour fever, other infections.
- TLS — red flags:
  - Recent cytotoxic therapy or high-grade haematologic malignancy + electrolyte abnormalities (K↑, PO4↑, uric acid↑, Ca↓) or oliguria/AKI.
  - Differentials: rhabdomyolysis, kidney injury from sepsis, other metabolic causes.
- CINV — recognition:
  - Temporal relation to chemo: acute (within 24 h), delayed (>24 h), anticipatory. Assess regimen emetogenicity and prior response.
Arabic summary: راقب ألم الظهر المتقدم، ضعف عصبي أو خلل بولي في MSCC؛ وورم/وذمة وجهية وصعوبة تنفس في SVCO؛ وحمى لدى متلقّي الكيماوي تعني تعفن محبّ للعدلات؛ واضطراب الإلكتروليتات بعد العلاج يلمّح إلى TLS.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- Immediate general tests (for most oncological emergencies): FBC, U&E, creatinine, Ca, PO4, Mg, uric acid (if TLS suspected), LFTs, lactate, blood cultures x2, ECG (if electrolytes abnormal), CXR if respiratory signs.
- MSCC:
  - Urgent MRI whole spine (not just symptomatic level). Do not delay steroid start if clinically indicated.
  - Plain XR/CT only if MRI unavailable; urgent neurosurgical/radiation oncology review.
- SVCO:
  - Contrast CT chest for diagnosis, level and cause; consider venography if planning stent.
- Neutropenic sepsis:
  - Blood cultures before antibiotics if does not delay therapy; urine culture, CXR if chest signs. Do not wait for neutrophil count to start antibiotics.
- TLS:
  - Serial electrolytes, renal function, uric acid, urine output, ECG monitoring for hyperkalaemia/hypocalcaemia changes.
- CINV:
  - Reconcile chemotherapy agents, previous antiemetic prophylaxis and timing (acute/delayed).
Arabic summary: فحوصات دم شاملة وثقافتان للدم قبل المضادات الحيوية إن أمكن؛ MRI كامل للعمود في MSCC؛ CT صدر في SVCO؛ مراقبة متسلسلة للكهارل في TLS؛ وسجل بروتوكولات مضادات الغثيان للجلسات السابقة.

4. Emergency management algorithms
- MSCC (time‑critical algorithm):
  1. Suspect MSCC → immediate high‑dose IV corticosteroid (start without waiting for MRI if high suspicion).
     - Common ED regimen used in exams: dexamethasone 16 mg IV stat, then 16 mg/day (verify local guideline) [NICE NG234].
  2. Urgent MRI whole spine while maintaining analgesia and hemodynamic stability.
  3. Urgent multidisciplinary discussion: neurosurgery vs. spinal oncology/radiation oncology → definitive therapy: surgical decompression (for radioresistant disease, spinal instability) or urgent radiotherapy.
  4. Support: bladder catheterisation if retention, physiotherapy, venous thromboprophylaxis as appropriate.
- SVCO (algorithm):
  1. Assess airway/oxygenation; sit patient upright; give oxygen and analgesia.
  2. Give corticosteroid for symptomatic relief (e.g., dexamethasone) if malignancy suspected.
  3. Obtain urgent contrast chest CT; oncology/radiology consult.
  4. Definitive: tumour-directed therapy (radiotherapy for radiosensitive tumours; chemotherapy for chemosensitive tumours). Endovascular stenting for severe airway compromise or rapid progression, or when diagnosis pending.
  5. Do not anticoagulate empirically unless thrombosis proven.
- Neutropenic sepsis (1‑hour rule):
  1. Any febrile chemotherapy patient → treat as neutropenic sepsis.
  2. Take blood cultures x2 and other relevant samples if does not delay care.
  3. Start IV broad-spectrum anti‑pseudomonal antibiotic within 1 hour (common first-line: piperacillin–tazobactam; meropenem for allergy/resistance — follow local policy) [NICE CKS].
  4. Give IV fluids, oxygen, monitor MAP/urine output; escalate per sepsis criteria; admit and involve oncology/haematology.
- TLS (recognition + management):
  1. Risk stratify pre-treatment; high-risk patients need prophylaxis.
  2. Established/suspected TLS: aggressive IV fluids (to maintain urine output), continuous cardiac monitoring.
  3. Manage hyperkalaemia per emergency hyperkalaemia protocols (insulin+glucose, beta-agonist, consider calcium if ECG changes), treat phosphate with binders, avoid routine calcium replacement unless symptomatic/ECG-indicated.
  4. Consider rasburicase for high-risk/established TLS; check G6PD before use when possible (contraindicated in G6PD deficiency).
  5. Renal replacement therapy if refractory hyperkalaemia, severe AKI or fluid overload.
- CINV (practical algorithm):
  1. Identify emetogenicity of regimen and previous control.
  2. Prophylaxis: acute — 5‑HT3 antagonist (ondansetron); add dexamethasone and NK1 antagonist (aprepitant) for highly emetogenic regimens.
  3. Treat breakthrough: use antiemetic of a different class (metoclopramide, prochlorperazine, olanzapine per local protocols).
Arabic summary: خطوات قابلة للتطبيق:
- MSCC: ديكساميثازون فورًا → MRI كامل → جراحة أو إشعاع عاجل.
- SVCO: وضع رأسي، أكسجين، ستيرويد، CT صدر ثم علاج موجه/دعامة إذا لزم.
- تعفن محبّ للعدلات: ثقافتان + مضاد حيوي واسع الطيف داخل ساعة (مثلاً بيبيراسيلين/تازوباكتام).
- TLS: سوائل متسلسلة، مراقبة كهارل، rasburicase للحالات عالية الخطر مع فحص G6PD.
- CINV: بروتوكول مضادات القئ حسب درجة التسبب بالغثيان.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
- Key drugs and pragmatic notes:
  - Dexamethasone: commonly used in MSCC/SVCO (exam regimen often quoted: 16 mg IV stat then 16 mg/day — verify local guidance) [NICE NG234].
  - Empiric antibiotics for neutropenic sepsis: start within 1 hour; common choice piperacillin–tazobactam (or meropenem for allergy/high resistance) — follow local antimicrobial policy [NICE CKS].
  - TLS: rasburicase for high-risk or established TLS; allopurinol for prophylaxis in lower‑risk patients. Check G6PD before rasburicase where possible.
  - Hyperkalaemia: emergency measures per standard algorithms (insulin+glucose, nebulised salbutamol, consider IV calcium if ECG changes).
  - Antiemetics: ondansetron (5‑HT3), dexamethasone, aprepitant (NK1); olanzapine for refractory/anticipatory nausea in some protocols.
- Procedures:
  - MRI whole spine (urgent) for MSCC.
  - CT chest with contrast for SVCO; endovascular stenting if indicated.
  - Central venous access may be needed for IV therapy; avoid device insertion in active bacteraemia unless necessary.
  - ICU-level support: for haemodynamic instability, severe TLS requiring dialysis, respiratory failure.
- Disposition and follow-up:
  - Admit all patients with neutropenic sepsis, TLS, symptomatic SVCO and MSCC.
  - MSCC/SVCO require urgent oncology/haematology and specialist (neurosurgery/radiation/interventional radiology) referral and clear documentation of neurologic baseline.
  - Arrange inpatient oncology/haematology follow-up and outpatient rehabilitation/physiotherapy for MSCC survivors.
Arabic summary: أدوية رئيسية: ديكساميثازون (MSCC/SVCO)، مضاد حيوي واسع الطيف داخل ساعة لتعفن قلة العدلات، rasburicase في TLS مع فحص G6PD. الإجراءات: MRI كامل للعمود، CT صدر، دعامة وريدية عند الحاجة. قبول بالمستشفى دائمًا في الحالات المذكورة.

6. High-yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- MSCC:
  - Trick: MRI whole spine — don’t scan a single level; start steroids immediately if clinical suspicion high — timing matters for neurological outcome.
  - Pitfall: dismissing progressive neurological signs as chronic pain without imaging.
- Neutropenic sepsis:
  - Trick: any post‑chemotherapy fever = neutropenic sepsis until proven otherwise. Start antibiotics within 1 hour — do not delay waiting for neutrophil count or seniors.
  - Pitfall: choosing antibiotics without checking local resistance/allergy policy.
- TLS:
  - Trick: classic biochemical pattern — K↑, PO4↑, Ca↓ (secondary), uric acid↑. Think TLS with oliguria + rising creatinine after chemo.
  - Pitfall: giving calcium for asymptomatic hypocalcaemia (risk calcium‑phosphate precipitation). Rasburicase needs G6PD consideration.
- SVCO:
  - Trick: steroids give rapid symptomatic improvement in malignant oedema — buy time for definitive therapy, especially with lymphomas.
  - Pitfall: assuming anticoagulation is always indicated — don't empirically anticoagulate unless thrombotic cause proven.
- CINV:
  - Trick: acute CINV responds best to 5‑HT3 antagonists; refractory requires change to a different pharmacologic class (olanzapine useful).
  - Pitfall: repeating same-class antiemetic for breakthrough.
Arabic summary: نقاط الاختبار:
- MSCC: ديكساميثازون فورًا وMRI كامل.
- حمى بعد العلاج الكيميائي = مضاد حيوي فوري.
- TLS: تجنّب إعطاء الكالسيوم بدون مبرر؛ فكر في rasburicase بعد فحص G6PD.
- SVCO: الستيرويد مفيد مؤقتًا؛ تجنب ممانعة التثبيت دون تشخيص.

7. Arabic rapid-review summary (المراجعة السريعة بالعربية)
- MSCC: ألم ظهر متقدم + علامات عصبية → ديكساميثازون فورًا → MRI كامل → جراحة أو إشعاع عاجل.
- SVCO: وذمة وجهية/عنق، ضيق تنفس → اجلس مريضًا، أكسجين، ديكساميثازون، CT صدر، علاج موجه أو دعامة وريدية.
- Neutropenic sepsis: أي حمى بعد الكيماوي → ثقافتان + مضاد حيوي واسع داخل ساعة → قبول وإشراك الأورام/الدم.
- TLS: فرط K+، PO4، حمض اليوريك → سوائل، مراقبة كهارل، rasburicase إذا عالي الخطر، تجنّب كالسيوم إلا عند الحاجة.
- CINV: استخدم 5‑HT3 للحاد، أضف ديكساميثازون/NK1 حسب خطورة النظام، لعلاج كسر القئ استخدم فصلية دوائية مختلفة.
خلاصة: لا تؤخر العلاجات الطارئة انتظار نتائج المخبر؛ اعرف بروتوكول المستشفى.

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- (No explicit Q-numbers provided in supplied material.) Covered FRCEM-relevant concepts:
  - MSCC: recognition, MRI‑first principle, steroid timing/dosing, surgical vs radiotherapy choices.
  - SVCO: clinical features, CT diagnosis, steroid use, stenting indications.
  - Neutropenic (febrile) sepsis: definition, 1‑hour antibiotic rule, sample-taking, first-line antibiotic classes.
  - Tumour lysis syndrome: electrolyte pattern, acute management, rasburicase and G6PD considerations, dialysis indications.
  - CINV: acute/delayed/anticipatory types, prophylaxis regimens and breakthrough strategies.
  - Diagnostics: essential blood tests, cultures, ECG, imaging priorities.
  - Practical pitfalls and exam-focused mnemonics/tricks.
- Use this map to cross-reference FRCEM question bank topics (MSCC, SVCO, neutropenic sepsis, TLS, CINV, emergency imaging, sepsis antibiotics).

Sources to verify
- NICE Guideline NG234 (MSCC corticosteroids) [NICE NG234]
- NICE Clinical Knowledge Summary — Neutropenic sepsis management [NICE CKS]
- BMJ Best Practice — Tumour lysis syndrome [BMJ BestPractice]
- Royal College of Emergency Medicine (RCEM) guidance, European Resuscitation Council (ERC) and local oncology/haematology protocols for institution‑specific antibiotic and steroid dosing.

This chapter is study material only — adapt to local protocols and the latest national guidance.


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 19: NEPHROLOGY
============================================================================================

Chapter 19 — Nephrology (final)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Acute kidney injury (AKI): abrupt fall in GFR → rise in serum creatinine and/or fall in urine output. Use KDIGO criteria for diagnosis. [KDIGO 2012]  
  - KDIGO (concise): ↑ creatinine ≥26.5 μmol/L (0.3 mg/dL) within 48 h OR ≥1.5× baseline within 7 days OR urine <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 h.  
- AKI categories: pre‑renal (reduced perfusion), intrinsic (ATN, glomerulonephritis, AIN), post‑renal (obstruction). Pathophysiology drives initial management (restore perfusion vs treat inflammation vs relieve obstruction).  
- Nephritic syndrome: haematuria (cola/tea urine), RBC casts, hypertension, oedema, reduced urine output — immune/inflammatory glomerular injury (e.g., post‑streptococcal GN).  
- Nephrotic syndrome: heavy proteinuria → hypoalbuminaemia, severe peripheral/anasarca, hyperlipidaemia; urine often “foamy.”  
- Rhabdomyolysis: muscle breakdown → myoglobinuria → direct tubular injury + renal vasoconstriction → AKI risk. CK very elevated; dipstick + blood but no RBCs.  
- Renal replacement therapy (RRT) indicated for life‑threatening complications summated by AEIOU: Acidosis, Electrolytes (refractory hyperkalaemia), Intoxications (dialysable), Overload (refractory), Uraemia (pericarditis/encephalopathy).

ملخص بالعرَبيّة: الفشل الكلوي الحاد = نقصان مفاجئ في وظيفة الكُلى؛ التمييز بين أسباب قبل كلوية/كلوية داخلية/بعد كلوية يوجّه العلاج. متلازمة نفريتية = بول دموي/ضغط مرتفع؛ نفروتية = بروتينية شديدة. اعتلال الربد العضلي يسبب بول داكن وCK مرتفع ويهدد بالفشل الكلوي. AEIOU لتحديد الحاجة إلى غسيل.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
Recognition
- Presentations: oliguria/anuria, non‑oliguric AKI, fluid overload (pulmonary oedema), confusion, nausea, arrhythmia (hyperkalaemia).  
- Red flags prompting urgent action: ECG changes of hyperkalaemia, refractory pulmonary oedema, uremic pericarditis/encephalopathy, rapidly rising creatinine, bilateral obstruction, anuria with suspected obstruction.

Key differentials
- Pre‑renal: dehydration, haemorrhage, sepsis, reduced cardiac output, drugs (ACEi/ARB/NSAID).  
- Intrinsic: acute tubular necrosis (ischaemic/toxic), glomerulonephritis (nephritic picture), acute interstitial nephritis (fever, rash, eosinophilia, drugs).  
- Post‑renal: bladder outlet obstruction, bilateral ureteric obstruction — consider prostate disease, stones, external compression.

Red‑flag exam clues
- Cola/tea urine after sore throat → post‑strep GN (nephritic).  
- Massive oedema + foamy urine → nephrotic syndrome.  
- Dipstick “blood” + few/no RBCs on microscopy → myoglobinuria from rhabdomyolysis.  
- Rapidly progressive oliguria with hyperkalaemia and ECG changes → urgent RRT consideration.

الشرح بالعربية: علامات التحذير: تغيرات ECG بسبب فرط بوتاسيوم، وذمة رئوية مقاومة، التهاب التامور اليوريمى، انسداد ثنائي المجرى البولي. فرط الكرياتينين سريع أو انعدام التبول يستلزمان تقييم فوري.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
Immediate bedside
- ABCs; ECG early if hyperkalaemia suspected (peaked T, wide QRS).  
- Urine dipstick (blood, protein), bedside glucose. Insert urinary catheter if accurate hourly urine output needed.

Initial laboratory panel
- Serum: creatinine, urea, electrolytes (K+, Na+, Ca2+, PO4), venous/arterial blood gas if acidotic, CK, LFTs, CBC (look for eosinophilia), coagulation as indicated.  
- Urinalysis: dipstick + microscopy (RBC casts, WBCs, eosinophils), urine protein quantification (ACR/UPCR) where possible.  
- Urine indices: urine Na, FENa or FEUrea can help pre‑renal vs intrinsic but are unreliable after diuretics or in CKD — interpret cautiously.

Imaging and specialty tests
- Renal ultrasound urgently if obstruction suspected (hydronephrosis) or to look for chronicity (small echogenic kidneys).  
- Specific serology if GN suspected: ANCA, ANA, complement levels (C3/C4), anti‑GBM, anti‑streptococcal titres — arrange early but manage emergently if life‑threatening.  
- Rhabdomyolysis: CK (often >>1,000 U/L; markedly high values >5,000 U/L increase risk but thresholds vary), urinalysis showing dipstick blood without RBCs.

Interpretation pitfalls
- Don’t rely on a single creatinine without baseline.  
- FENa misleading after diuretics; CKD alters indices.  
- Dipstick cannot distinguish haemoglobin vs myoglobin vs RBCs; microscopy is essential.

الشرح بالعربية: فحص البول الشريطي سريع ومفيد لكن تأكّد من الميكروسكوب. تصوير بالموجات فوق الصوتية للكِلى ضروري عند الاشتباه بالانسداد. حسابات الـ FENa لا تُعتمد بعد استخدام المدرّات أو في CKD.

4. Emergency management algorithms
Algorithm A — AKI in ED (compact)
1. ABC → monitor ECG if K+ suspected high.  
2. Stop nephrotoxins (NSAIDs, ACEi/ARB, diuretics, aminoglycosides).  
3. Assess volume status:
   - Hypovolaemia → give isotonic crystalloid (bolus) and reassess.  
   - Euvolemic → search intrinsic causes (labs, urine studies, GN screen).  
   - Hypervolemia → oxygen, consider loop diuretic bolus/infusion if responsive; if refractory or life‑threatening pulmonary oedema → urgent RRT.  
4. Insert catheter for accurate urine output.  
5. Urgent nephrology if severe/uncertain cause or likely RRT.

Algorithm B — Life‑threatening hyperkalaemia
- If ECG changes or K+ markedly high: do steps in parallel
  1. Stabilise membrane: IV calcium gluconate 10 mL of 10% (1 g) IV over 2–5 min.  
  2. Shift K+ intracellularly: insulin 10 U IV + 25 g glucose IV (e.g., 50 mL 50%); consider nebulised salbutamol (10–20 mg) and IV bicarbonate if severe acidosis.  
  3. Remove K+: consider sodium polystyrene (limited role in emergencies) and arrange urgent RRT for refractory hyperkalaemia or very high K+ (esp. K+ >6.5 mmol/L or with ECG changes).  
  4. Continuous cardiac monitoring; check K+ frequently.

Algorithm C — Rhabdomyolysis
1. Suspect: muscle pain/weakness + dark urine or dipstick blood without RBCs.  
2. Immediate actions: aggressive IV isotonic crystalloids — initial bolus (commonly 1–2 L unless contraindicated) then high‑rate infusion to maintain brisk urine output; insert catheter and monitor.  
3. Monitor CK, creatinine, electrolytes, urine colour/output; correct hyperkalaemia and acidosis per protocols.  
4. Consider nephrology if CK very high, rising creatinine, oliguria, hyperkalaemia, or severe acidosis. Bicarbonate or mannitol are controversial — consult nephrology.

Algorithm D — Obstruction
- If bladder outlet obstruction → catheterisation (urgent).  
- If suspected bilateral ureteric obstruction or solitary kidney obstruction → urgent urology ± decompression (stent/nephrostomy) and renal ultrasound.

Notes on RRT indications
- AEIOU mnemonic for urgent dialysis: Acidosis (severe/refractory), Electrolytes (refractory hyperkalaemia), Intoxications (dialysable), Overload (refractory pulmonary oedema), Uraemia (pericarditis/encephalopathy). Clinical context and nephrology input determine timing.

الشرح بالعربية: خوارزميات سريعة — وقف السموم الكلوية، تقييم السوائل وتعديلها، مراقبة البول وECG، علاج فرط بوتاسيوم طارئ (كالسيوم، أنسولين+غلوكوز)، وبدء سوائل قوية في الربد العضلي. انسداد الحالب يحتاج تفريغ عاجل.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
Drugs and doses (well established)
- Calcium gluconate 10 mL of 10% (1 g) IV over 2–5 min — for ECG protection in hyperkalaemia.  
- Insulin for hyperkalaemia: 10 U regular IV + 25 g glucose IV.  
- Nebulised salbutamol 10–20 mg can shift K+ intracellularly (adjunct).  
- Stop nephrotoxins immediately (NSAIDs, ACEi/ARB, diuretics, aminoglycosides).

Procedures
- Urinary catheter for output measurement and bladder decompression.  
- Renal ultrasound for obstruction or chronicity.  
- Urgent urology for decompression (stent/nephrostomy) if obstruction not relieved by catheter.  
- Vascular access for dialysis/CRRT if RRT anticipated (early nephrology/renal unit involvement).

Disposition and follow‑up
- Admit all patients with AKI except mild, clearly pre‑renal reversible cases with outpatient follow‑up.  
- Urgent nephrology review for: rapidly rising creatinine, oliguria/anuria, refractory hyperkalaemia, severe acidosis, suspected GN, need for RRT, or uncertainty.  
- Arrange outpatient renal follow‑up if AKI resolves but baseline abnormality or persistent proteinuria/haematuria.  
- Document and communicate medication changes (hold ACEi/ARBs/NSAIDs) and reason for hold to primary care.

Special notes
- Bicarbonate or mannitol for rhabdomyolysis: evidence inconsistent — use after nephrology advice.  
- Avoid automatic numeric cutoffs for dialysis (use AEIOU + clinical picture). pH thresholds for dialysis vary by guideline; clarify local practice.

الشرح بالعربية: جرعات رئيسية: كالسيوم جلوكونات 1 غرام IV لعلاج فرط بوتاسيوم الحاد؛ أنسولين 10U + 25 غرام سكر لنقل البوتاسيوم داخل الخلايا. قم بوضع قسطرة بولية وموجات فوق صوت للكِلى عند الاشتباه في انسداد. راجع الكُلى عاجلاً عند حالات شديدة أو حاجة لغسيل الكِلى.

6. High‑yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
High‑yield tricks
- KDIGO quick recall: 0.3 mg/dL (26.5 μmol/L) rise in 48 h or 1.5× baseline in 7 days or urine <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 h. Exam favourite. [KDIGO 2012]  
- AEIOU mnemonic = dialysis indications — classic exam staple.  
- Dipstick “blood” + no RBCs on microscopy = myoglobinuria (rhabdomyolysis).  
- Cola/tea‑coloured urine after sore throat = post‑streptococcal GN (nephritic).  
- Foamy urine + heavy proteinuria = nephrotic syndrome.  
- Hyperkalaemia immediate sequence: Calcium → Insulin+glucose → Removal (nebulised salbutamol, bicarbonate if acidotic, RRT). In exams, remember calcium first for ECG changes.

Common pitfalls
- Overreliance on single creatinine value without baseline—may miss AKI or misclassify CKD.  
- Using FENa after diuretics or in CKD — can be misleading. FEUrea may be more useful with diuretics but still imperfect.  
- Assuming fixed numeric dialysis cutoffs (e.g., creatinine > X) — context matters; AEIOU + nephrology.  
- Forgetting to stop nephrotoxins early (NSAIDs, ACEi/ARBs) — common cause of preventable AKI.  
- Treating hyperkalaemia without continuous ECG monitoring or without definitive potassium removal when indicated.

نصائح للامتحان بالعربية: تذكّر رقم 0.3 mg/dL في تعريف KDIGO، AEIOU للغسيل، وُجهة العلاج لفرط بوتاسيوم: كالسيوم ثم أنسولين+جلوكوز ثم إزالة/غسيل. لا تعتمد على FENa بعد المدرّات.

7. Arabic rapid‑review summary (Modern Standard Arabic)
- تعريف: الفشل الكلوي الحاد = ارتفاع مفاجئ في الكرياتينين أو نقصان في إخراج البول؛ استخدم معايير KDIGO.  
- الأسباب: قبل كلوية (نقص تروية/أدوية)، داخلية (ATN، التهاب كبيبات)، بعد كلوية (انسداد).  
- علامات حمراء: تغيرات ECG بفرط بوتاسيوم، وذمة رئوية مقاومة، التهاب غشاء التامور اليوريمى، انسداد ثنائي.  
- فحوصات عاجلة: ECG، تحليل بول شريطي + ميكروسكوب، كرياتينين/إلكتروليتات، CK، تصوير كِلى بالموجات فوق الصوتية عند اشتباه انسداد.  
- علاج طارئ: أوقف السموم الكلوية، عدّل حالة السوائل، غلوبولات/أنسولين+غلوكوز لفرط بوتاسيوم؛ سوائل قوية للربد العضلي؛ تصريف انسداد فوراً.  
- إحالة عاجلة للكُلى عند شدة الحالة أو احتمال الحاجة لغسيل.

خلاصة قصيرة: قيّم ABC، أوقف المسببات، عالج فرط بوتاسيوم وفق الأولوية (كالسيوم → أنسولين+غلوكوز → إزالة)، واطلب مساعدة الكُلى مبكراً.

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- AKI definition and KDIGO criteria; urine output thresholds.  
- AKI aetiology: pre‑renal vs intrinsic vs post‑renal; drug causes (NSAIDs, ACEi/ARB).  
- Initial ED workup: urine dipstick/microscopy, electrolytes, creatinine, CK, ECG, renal ultrasound.  
- Use and limitations of urine indices (FENa/FEUrea).  
- Hyperkalaemia recognition and emergency treatment sequence (calcium, insulin+glucose, salbutamol, RRT indications).  
- AEIOU indications for dialysis and caveats about numeric cutoffs.  
- Rhabdomyolysis: diagnosis (dipstick blood/no RBCs, CK), immediate aggressive IV fluids, monitoring, nephrology referral.  
- Obstructive uropathy management: catheterisation, urology decompression.  
- Nephritic vs nephrotic clinical clues.  
- Practical ED algorithms for AKI, hyperkalaemia, rhabdomyolysis, obstruction.  
- Exam tricks: KDIGO 0.3 mg/dL rule, dipstick vs microscopy, AEIOU mnemonic, FENa pitfalls.

Sources to verify
- KDIGO Clinical Practice Guideline for Acute Kidney Injury (2012).  
- McMahon score literature for rhabdomyolysis risk stratification.  
- Local and national renal/dialysis protocols; RCEM, ERC, NICE renal and hyperkalaemia guidance as applicable.

(Study material only — not individual patient advice.)


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 20: MUSCULOSKELETAL (NON-TRAUMATIC)
============================================================================================

Chapter 20: Musculoskeletal (non‑traumatic)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Common ED categories: inflammatory (septic arthritis, gout/pseudogout, bursitis, septic bursitis, tenosynovitis), overuse/tendinopathy (lateral/medial epicondylitis, De Quervain, rotator cuff), systemic inflammatory myopathies/PMR, degenerative/fragility (osteoarthritis, osteoporosis, adhesive capsulitis), neuropathic/entrapment (radial tunnel, PIN palsy, carpal tunnel).  
- Pathophysiology focus:
  - Septic arthritis: bacterial invasion → synovial inflammation, purulence, cartilage destruction; rapid diagnosis essential to prevent irreversible joint damage and sepsis.
  - Crystal arthropathies: monosodium urate (gout) or calcium pyrophosphate (pseudogout) provoke neutrophil‑mediated synovitis; crystals may coexist with infection.
  - Tenosynovitis/hand infections: confined sheath/space infection → compartment‑like pressure, tendon ischemia, potential flexor tendon necrosis; Kanavel signs indicate flexor sheath involvement.
  - Tendinopathy/overuse: microtears, degenerative tendinosis, peritendinous inflammation; mechanical load and repetitive use predominate.
  - Adhesive capsulitis: capsular fibrosis with global loss of capsular volume → progressive active and passive ROM restriction.

المفهوم والفيزيولوجيا المرضية (عربي مختصر)
- حالات الطوارئ: العدوى داخل المفصل وغمد الأوتار واحتشاء اليد ونقائل العمود الفقري. الآليات تشمل التهاب قيحي للمفصل، التهاب بلّوري بلّوري بالنقش الكريستالي، وضمور ليفي للمفصل (الكتف المتجمد).

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Monoarticular hot swollen joint: always exclude septic arthritis first. Red flags → fever, rigors, severe pain, inability to weight‑bear (large joint), systemic sepsis signs. DDx: septic arthritis vs gout/pseudogout vs haemarthrosis vs acute osteomyelitis adjacent to joint.
- Hand/phalangeal infections: paronychia, felon, pyogenic flexor tenosynovitis (Kanavel’s 4 signs: fusiform swelling, tenderness along flexor sheath, pain on passive extension, flexed posture). Any Kanavel sign → urgent surgical/hand consult.
- Shoulder pain:
  - Painful arc 60–120° → subacromial pathology (supraspinatus tendinopathy/impingement).  
  - Global active + passive restriction → adhesive capsulitis.  
  - Bilateral proximal stiffness with raised inflammatory markers → polymyalgia rheumatica (PMR); screen for GCA symptoms.
- Elbow/wrist distinctions:
  - Lateral epicondylitis (tennis elbow): point tenderness at lateral epicondyle, pain on resisted wrist extension.  
  - Radial tunnel: more diffuse pain 3–5 cm distal; pain on resisted middle‑finger extension; PIN palsy causes motor weakness without sensory loss.  
  - De Quervain: radial‑styloid pain, positive Finkelstein (thumb inside fist + ulnar deviation). Intersection syndrome = painful crepitus 4–6 cm proximal, different location.
- Red flags across MSK: rapidly progressive weakness, neurovascular compromise, overlying skin infection with spreading cellulitis, systemic sepsis, back pain with neurological deficits or cancer history (spinal metastasis/cord compression).

التعرّف والتمييز والعلامات الحمراء (عربي مختصر)
- مفصل أحادي ساخن → استبعد التهاب مفصلي صديدي.  
- علامات كانافيل → استدعاء جراحة يد عاجل.  
- تمييز دقيق لموقع الألم في الرسغ/المرفق يساعد التفرقة بين دقرفان، intersection syndrome، ومتلازمة النفق الشعاعي.  
- فكر بـ PMR وGCA في كبار السن مع ألم كتف/حوض صباحي.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- General principles: focused history (onset, trauma, systemic features, immunosuppression, joint prosthesis, animal/human bite), targeted exam tests (Kanavel, Finkelstein, painful arc, Neer/Hawkins, Empty‑can, lift‑off), directed baseline labs and imaging.
- Acute hot joint algorithm:
  1) Rapid assessment (vitals, sepsis screen).  
  2) Blood cultures if febrile/septic.  
  3) Urgent arthrocentesis BEFORE antibiotics when feasible — send Gram stain, cell count with differential, culture, crystal analysis, glucose, protein.
