- ارتفاعات: أسيتازولاميد للوقاية؛ ديكساميثازون للـ HACE؛ نيفيديبين للـ HAPE — راجع إرشادات محلية للجرعات.

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- Diving: nitrogen narcosis, decompression sickness (DCS), arterial gas embolism (AGE), pulmonary/inner‑ear barotrauma.
- Heat illness: heat exhaustion, exertional heat stroke, classic heat stroke, rhabdomyolysis complications.
- Electrical and lightning injuries: arrhythmias, keraunoparalysis, rhabdomyolysis, burns.
- Inhalational toxins: carbon monoxide poisoning (COHb interpretation), smoke inhalation, cyanide (hydroxocobalamin).
- HAZMAT/chemical: decontamination principles (hot/warm/cold zones, dry vs wet decon), PPE.
- Radiation: acute radiation syndrome basics, lymphocyte kinetics, decontamination, KI for radioactive iodine per public‑health guidance.
- Blast injuries: primary blast effects (lungs/ears/GI), observation principles.
- Altitude medicine: AMS, HACE, HAPE — acetazolamide, dexamethasone, nifedipine and adjuncts.
- Diagnostics and pitfalls: CO‑oximetry vs pulse oximetry, CK/renal monitoring for rhabdo, imaging for barotrauma/neurology.
- Practical emergency algorithms: immediate O2, scene safety, urgent hyperbaric referral, rapid cooling for heat stroke, hydroxocobalamin for suspected cyanide.

Final checks / flagged items (verify locally)
- Pediatric acetazolamide dosing cited in some sources — confirm with paediatric guidelines.
- Sildenafil/tadalafil roles and exact dosing for HAPE prevention/treatment — evidence variable; verify specialty guidance.
- Exact numeric COHb thresholds for hyperbaric oxygen vary between centres — follow local hyperbaric protocols.
- Positioning for gas embolism (left lateral/Trendelenburg) is traditional; modern emphasis is immediate 100% O2 and urgent recompression — verify local resuscitation guidance.

Sources to verify
- RCEM Learning, CDC Yellow Book 2024 (altitude guidance), Cyanokit (hydroxocobalamin), RCEM/UK, European Resuscitation Council (ERC), NICE, local hyperbaric centre protocols, national HAZMAT and public‑health guidance.


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 26: GASTROENTEROLOGY & HEPATOLOGY
============================================================================================

Chapter 26: Gastroenterology & Hepatology

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Upper GI bleeding (UGIB)
  - Common causes: peptic ulcer disease (most common), variceal haemorrhage (portal hypertension, cirrhosis), Mallory‑Weiss tear, oesophagitis. Pathophysiology: mucosal erosion, portal‑hypertension variceal rupture.
  - Risk stratify with Glasgow‑Blatchford Score (GBS).
- Variceal haemorrhage
  - Portal hypertension → variceal formation and rupture; large-volume haematemesis, high rebleeding/mortality risk.
  - Management includes vasoactive therapy, antibiotics, urgent endoscopic therapy.
- Spontaneous bacterial peritonitis (SBP)
  - Gut bacterial translocation in cirrhosis with ascites (commonly E. coli). Diagnostic paracentesis essential.
- Ascending cholangitis
  - Biliary obstruction → infection (Charcot triad: RUQ pain, fever, jaundice; Reynolds pentad adds hypotension, altered mentation).
- Acute liver failure (ALF)
  - Rapid loss of hepatic function (coagulopathy, encephalopathy); paracetamol common cause.
- Metabolic/other hepatology emergencies
  - Alcoholic ketoacidosis (AKA): binge ethanol + starvation → high AG metabolic acidosis with ketones.
  - Refeeding syndrome: phosphate, magnesium, potassium shifts on re‑feeding malnourished patients.
  - C. difficile colitis: recent antibiotics → pseudomembranous colitis.
  - Oesophageal disease: eosinophilic oesophagitis (progressive dysphagia), candida (odynophagia in immunosuppressed).
  - Food bolus impaction: commonly meat bolus in oesophagus; risk of complete obstruction/aspiration.

موجز سريري/فيزياء المرض
- نزف علوي: القرحة الشائعة؛ دوالي المريء نتيجة ارتفاع الضغط البابي.
- SBP: عبور بكتيري من الأمعاء إلى البريتوني.
- التهاب القناة الصفراوية: انسداد مُعدٍ للقناة الصفراوية.
- الفشل الكبدي الحاد: فقد سريع للوظيفة مع اضطراب تخثر واعتلال دماغي.
- متلازمات أيضية: AKA وإعادة التغذية وفيروس/بكتيريا معوية مميزة.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- UGIB recognition
  - Presentation: haematemesis ± melaena, syncope, hypotension, tachycardia. Red flags: haemodynamic instability, ongoing large‑volume haematemesis, fresh PR bleeding suggesting massive bleeding.
  - Differentiate variceal vs non‑variceal: cirrhosis/alcohol/portal HTN → variceal likely.
- SBP
  - Suspect in any cirrhotic with ascites + fever, abdominal pain, encephalopathy, unexplained deterioration. Red flags: peritonism, severe focal pain → consider secondary peritonitis/surgical abdomen.
- Cholangitis
  - Suspect with RUQ pain + fever ± jaundice. Red flags: hypotension and confusion (Reynolds pentad) → septic shock, urgent drainage.
- ALF
  - New encephalopathy + coagulopathy after acute insult. Red flags: grade 3–4 encephalopathy, rapid INR rise, acidosis.
- Other red flags
  - Food bolus with drooling/complete obstruction → urgent airway/endoscopy.
  - Massive haematemesis → early intubation for airway protection.
  - Refeeding: early hypophosphataemia, arrhythmias/seizures.

التعرّف والتفرقة والعلامات الحمراء
- علامات حمراء: نزيف كبير، اختلال ضغط الدم، اعتلال دماغي كبدي حاد، صدمة إنتانية في التهاب القناة الصفراوية، ألم بطن بموضع محدد (استبعد التهاب بطني ثانوي).

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- General priorities: ABC → monitor; targeted history (cirrhosis, NSAIDs, alcohol, varices, recent antibiotics, foreign‑body ingestion).
- UGIB
  - Immediate: two large‑bore IVs, type & cross if significant bleed, labs (Hb, platelets, coagulation, urea, LFTs, group & save), GBS for risk stratification (GBS = 0 → consider outpatient management).
  - Imaging/endoscopy: urgent endoscopy for unstable or suspected variceal bleed (within 12 h). Stable non‑variceal: endoscopy within 24 h [NICE CG141].
  - Note: Urea rises with upper GI blood.
- SBP
  - Diagnostic paracentesis in ED for any cirrhotic with signs of infection or decompensation. Send cell count, culture, albumin. PMN ≥250 cells/mm3 diagnostic.
  - Differentiate from secondary peritonitis by focal peritonism, high neutrophil count, imaging.
- Cholangitis
  - Blood cultures, LFTs, WCC, bilirubin. Abdominal ultrasound to detect dilation; CT if diagnostic uncertainty. ERCP for therapy once stabilised.
- ALF
  - Labs: INR, bilirubin, creatinine, arterial blood gas, lactate, ammonia, paracetamol level. Apply King's College Criteria for transplant need.
- Other diagnoses
  - C. difficile: stool testing and clinical severity guide therapy; recent antibiotic exposure is key.
  - Oesophageal candida: visual/clinical diagnosis; endoscopy if atypical.
  - Food bolus: bedside assessment (can patient swallow saliva?), lateral neck/chest X‑ray rarely helpful — endoscopy if complete obstruction or failed conservative measures.

التشخيص والتفسيرات
- خط أساس: ABC ثم فِحوصات موجهة. SBP يُشخّص عبر بزل البطن وPMN≥250. جراحية إذ وُجد ألم موضعي شديد. قيّم حاجة زرع للكبد باستخدام معايير King's College.

4. Emergency management algorithms
- UGIB (concise algorithm)
  1. ABC, cardiac monitoring, two large bore IVs, resuscitate with crystalloids.
  2. Activate transfusion/major haemorrhage protocol if unstable or ongoing loss.
  3. Restrictive transfusion strategy: transfuse if Hb <7 g/dL (higher threshold if ischaemic heart disease) — follow local policy.
  4. If cirrhosis/variceal suspicion:
     - Start IV antibiotics (e.g., ceftriaxone per local guidance) AND vasoactive therapy (terlipressin or local equivalent) immediately.
     - Arrange urgent endoscopy (within 12 h).
  5. If massive haematemesis and airway at risk: intubate for airway protection before endoscopy or Sengstaken‑Blakemore insertion.
  6. Balloon tamponade (Sengstaken‑Blakemore) only as a temporising bridge to definitive therapy:
     - Insert orally with patient intubated; inflate gastric balloon first; oesophageal balloon inflation ~30–40 mmHg; remove ≤24–48 h; arrange transfer to definitive care.
  7. For coagulopathy in major bleed: FFP 15 mL/kg for INR correction during massive haemorrhage.
  8. Routine TXA not recommended for UGIB (HALT‑IT trial) [HALT‑IT 2020].
- SBP
  1. Perform paracentesis promptly (before antibiotics if possible).
  2. If clinically suspected, start empiric IV third‑generation cephalosporin (e.g., ceftriaxone) pending results.
  3. Consider albumin infusion per local guideline in high‑risk patients (verify hospital protocol).
- Ascending cholangitis
  1. Resuscitate, fluids, broad‑spectrum IV antibiotics covering gram negatives/anaerobes.
  2. Early biliary decompression (ERCP) once patient stabilised; urgent if severe/septic.
- Acute liver failure (paracetamol)
  1. Admit to critical care; start hepatoprotective measures (e.g., N‑acetylcysteine per local protocol).
  2. Apply King's College Criteria; discuss urgent transplant referral when criteria met.
- Alcoholic ketoacidosis
  1. IV crystalloid resuscitation, high‑dose thiamine before glucose, correct electrolytes, monitor acid–base.
- Refeeding syndrome prevention
  1. Identify high‑risk patients, start low calories, give thiamine, and monitor/replace phosphate, magnesium, potassium closely.
- Food bolus impaction
  1. If able to manage secretions → conservative escalation: carbonated drink trial → antispasmodic (hyoscine/Buscopan) → consider glucagon/GTN/nifedipine trial (variable efficacy).
  2. If complete obstruction, drooling, or failed conservative measures → urgent endoscopy.

خوارزميات علاجية طارئة
- نزيف علوي: ABC → استقرار دوراني → مضادات حيوية + عامل وعائي لدوالي → منظار عاجل. لا تُستخدم أنبوب Sengstaken إلا كجسر بعد تنبيب المريض.
- SBP: بزل ثم مضاد حيوي ثالث الجيل عاجل.
- التهاب القناة: سوائل ومضادّات حيوية ثم تفريغ القناة (ERCP) سريعًا.
- فشل كبدي حاد: NAC واعتبارات زرع حسب معايير King's College.
- إعادة التغذية: بدء متدرّج ومراقبة الفسفور والماغنسيوم والبوتاسيوم.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
- Key drugs/principles
  - Vasoactive agents for variceal bleed: terlipressin (local dosing protocols vary) — start early.
  - Antibiotics for cirrhotic UGIB: ceftriaxone commonly used; adjust for local resistance.
  - IV third‑generation cephalosporin for suspected SBP (e.g., ceftriaxone).
  - N‑acetylcysteine for paracetamol ALF per local protocol.
  - Oral vancomycin or fidaxomicin for C. difficile per local guideline.
  - Thiamine before glucose in malnourished/alcoholic patients.
- Key procedures
  - Diagnostic paracentesis (ED) for ascites + infection suspicion.
  - Urgent endoscopy (OGD) for major UGIB or failed conservative management.
  - ERCP for biliary decompression in cholangitis.
  - Sengstaken‑Blakemore tube as temporary measure for refractory variceal bleeding (intubate first).
  - Consider interventional radiology (TIPS or embolisation) if endoscopy fails for variceal or non‑variceal bleeding.
- Disposition and follow‑up
  - Admit unstable UGIB, variceal bleeds, SBP, cholangitis, ALF to appropriate monitored bed/critical care.
  - GBS = 0 may be considered for outpatient management with prompt endoscopy arrangement.
  - SBP: hospital admission, complete antibiotic course, follow ascitic fluid cultures; consider secondary prophylaxis.
  - Cholangitis: admission, definitive ERCP, follow-up hepatobiliary surgery/gastroenterology.
  - ALF: critical care + early transplant centre referral if criteria met.
  - Food bolus managed successfully: arrange gastroenterology follow‑up and investigation for underlying oesophageal disease (e.g., EoE, strictures).
- Practical notes
  - Confirm local terlipressin/antibiotic dosing and PPI policies; hospital protocols vary.
  - For coagulopathy in massive bleed, use FFP 15 mL/kg; consider PCC for warfarin reversal as per guideline.

الأدوية والإجراءات والمآل والمتابعة
- أدوية أساسية: تيرليبريسين للدوالي، سيفترياكسون لـSBP/وقاية نزفيّة لدى تليفى الكبد، NAC لفشل كبدي دوائي.
- إجراءات: بزل البطن تشخيصي، منظار عاجل، ERCP للتفريغ الصفراوي، Sengstaken كحل مؤقت بعد تنبيب.
- التنويم: حالات خطرة أو فشل علاجي—وضع مراقبة/عناية مركزة، إحالة مبكرة لمراكز الزرع عند الحاجة.

6. High-yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- Paracentesis rule: any cirrhotic with ascites + fever/abdominal pain/encephalopathy → do paracentesis (exam classic).
- GBS trick: GBS = 0 is the classic safe outpatient discharge answer in exam vignettes [NICE CG141].
- Sengstaken tube pitfalls: never insert without securing airway — always intubate first in massive haematemesis scenarios.
- Variceal bleed bundle: antibiotic + vasoactive agent + urgent endoscopy — omission is a common negative exam trap.
- TXA in UGIB: HALT‑IT trial showed no routine benefit — answer exam questions asserting “not recommended routinely.” [HALT‑IT 2020]
- FFP vs PCC: FFP 15 mL/kg used in massive bleed coagulopathy; PCC used for rapid warfarin reversal per local policy — read the vignette for anticoagulant context.
- PPI pre‑endoscopy: guideline variability—NICE does not recommend routine IV PPI for undifferentiated UGIB; exam distractors may exploit this — check stem clues for suspected non‑variceal bleed.
- Refeeding syndrome: phosphate falls first — do not wait for severe symptoms; monitor and replace early.
- Food bolus: attempt conservative measures only if no airway compromise; exam will often have drooling/stridor as red flags for immediate endoscopy.
- Alcoholic ketoacidosis vs DKA: exam distinction relies on history (alcohol, starvation) and relatively low glucose in AKA; management prioritises IV fluids and thiamine.
- ALF transplant threshold: remember King's College Criteria — pH <7.3 single criterion for paracetamol ALF; else combination of INR/creatinine/encephalopathy.

نصائح للامتحان ومزالق شائعة
- بزل البطن دائمًا عند الاشتباه بـSBP في مريض تليفي.
- GBS=0 إجابة شائعة للمناظير الخارجيّة.
- تنبيب المجرى التنفسي قبل أي محاولة لإدخال أنبوب Tamponade.
- HALT‑IT: لا تعتمد على TXA روتينيًا في النزف الهضمي العلوي.

7. Arabic rapid-review summary (مراجعة سريعة بالعربية)
- نزف علوي: قيّم ABC؛ استخدم GBS؛ دوالي → مضادّ حيوي + تيرليبريسين + منظار عاجل. (NICE CG141)
- SBP: بزل البطن تشخيصي؛ PMN ≥250 خلية/مم3؛ ابدأ سيفترياكسون مبدئيًا.
- التهاب القنوات الصفراوية: حمى + ألم RUQ + يرقان → سوائل، مضادّات حيوية ثم ERCP للتفريغ.
- فشل كبدي حاد بعد باراسيتامول: تطبيق معايير King's College → إحالة زرع مبكرة.
- متلازمة إعادة التغذية: راقب واستبدل الفسفور والماغنسيوم والبوتاسيوم؛ أعطِ الثيامين قبل الجلوكوز.
- انسداد طعام بالمريء: جرّب مشروبات غازية، مرخِّيات؛ منظار عاجل إذا فشل أو اكتمال انسداد.
- تحذيرات: لا تُدخل Sengstaken دون تنبيب؛ TXA ليس روتينياً في UGIB (HALT‑IT).

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- UGIB & risk stratification: GBS, transfusion strategy, major haemorrhage, endoscopy timing (concepts often in Qs on UGIB/upper GI haemorrhage).
- Variceal haemorrhage: antibiotics, vasoactive therapy, urgent endoscopy, Sengstaken tamponade, airway protection.
- SBP: diagnostic paracentesis, PMN ≥250, empiric third‑generation cephalosporin.
- Ascending cholangitis: Charcot triad, Reynolds pentad, resuscitation + ERCP.
- Acute liver failure (paracetamol): King's College Criteria, transplant referral, NAC.
- Alcoholic ketoacidosis vs DKA: recognition and initial management (fluids, thiamine).
- Refeeding syndrome: phosphate‑centric electrolyte management.
- C. difficile colitis: recognition and first‑line agents (vancomycin/fidaxomicin per local guideline).
- Oesophageal disorders & food bolus: eosinophilic oesophagitis, candida, conservative escalation, endoscopy indications.
- Coagulopathy in bleeding: FFP dosing during massive haemorrhage, PCC vs FFP considerations.
- Exam/pitfall topics: TXA (HALT‑IT), PPI pre‑endoscopy variability, Sengstaken tube airway rule.

خريطة تغطية الأسئلة (المفاهيم)
- تشمل كل مفاهيم النزف العلوي، دوالي المريء، SBP، التهاب القناة الصفراوية، الفشل الكبدي الحاد، متلازمات أيضية مهمة، ومداخلات الطوارئ الأساسية.

Sources to verify
- NICE CG141 (upper GI bleeding), King's College criteria for paracetamol ALF, HALT‑IT trial (Lancet 2020) re: TXA, local hospital protocols for terlipressin/antibiotics/ERCP, RCEM/ED/critical‑care transfer pathways, BSG/EASL guidance where applicable.

(Study note: This chapter is for exam preparation and general emergency management principles. Always follow local protocols and consult specialist services for individual patient care.)


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 27: ENDOCRINOLOGY
============================================================================================

Chapter 27 — Endocrinology (compact FRCEM final chapter)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Core idea: endocrine emergencies present as metabolic instability ± volume/electrolyte disturbance, acute neuro or cardiovascular compromise. Rapid recognition and hormone replacement/supportive care are often life‑saving.
- Key syndromes: adrenal crisis (primary/secondary), diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) including euglycaemic DKA (SGLT2i), hyperosmolar hyperglycaemic state (HHS), severe hyponatraemia / SIADH, central diabetes insipidus (post‑op), pituitary apoplexy, myxoedema coma, thyroid storm, pheochromocytoma crisis, severe hypercalcaemia, toxic high anion gap acidoses (eg. pyroglutamic), toxic alcohols.
- Physiologic rules to memorise:
  - Primary adrenal insufficiency → hypotension + hyponatraemia + hyperkalaemia.
  - DKA → high anion gap metabolic acidosis, ketonaemia, total body K+ depleted despite serum K+ often normal/high initially.
  - SIADH → hypotonic euvolemic hyponatraemia with inappropriately concentrated urine (Uosm >100) and urine Na >30–40 mmol/L.
  - Central DI → large polyuria (>3 L/day), hyperosmolar plasma, dilute urine.
  - Pituitary apoplexy → sudden severe headache, visual field loss, ocular palsies, hypotension from secondary adrenal failure.
- Exam relevance: fluid/electrolyte patterns + specific triggers (surgery, infection, drugs like SGLT2i or DDAVP) point to diagnosis.

Arabic (مختصر): الطوارئ الهرمونية تسبب اضطراب استقلابى وحجم/إلكتروليتات أو اختلال عصبي/قلبي؛ التعرف السريع وتعويض الهرمونات ضروريان للنجاة.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Adrenal crisis:
  - Presentation: acute hypotension, vomiting, abdominal pain, weakness, confusion.
  - Key labs: hyponatraemia, hyperkalaemia (primary), very low random cortisol.
  - Differentials: sepsis, GI bleed, cardiac causes.
  - Red flag: shock unresponsive to fluids → give hydrocortisone immediately.
- DKA / euglycaemic DKA:
  - Presentation: polyuria, polydipsia, dehydration, Kussmaul breathing, abdominal pain, confusion.
  - DKA labs: ketonaemia/ketonuria, metabolic acidosis (pH <7.3 or HCO3 <15), hyperglycaemia (usually >11 mmol/L). Euglycaemic DKA: same acidosis + ketones with normal/modest glucose — suspect SGLT2 inhibitors.
  - Red flag: falling serum K+ after insulin → arrhythmia risk.
- HHS:
  - Very high glucose, hyperosmolarity, little/no ketones, severe dehydration, altered mental state.
- Hyponatraemia / SIADH:
  - Classify by tonicity and volume status. SIADH: euvolemic, hypotonic serum, Uosm >100, UNa >30–40, normal adrenal/thyroid.
  - Red flags: seizures, severe obtundation → urgent hypertonic therapy.
- Central DI:
  - Large-volume dilute urine, hypernatraemia/high plasma osmolality, recent pituitary surgery/trauma.
  - Red flag: rapid sodium change after desmopressin → monitor closely.
- Pituitary apoplexy:
  - Sudden severe headache, bitemporal field loss, ocular palsies, hypotension. Red flag: visual deterioration, shock → urgent steroids + neurosurgical review.
- Thyroid storm / myxoedema coma:
  - Thyroid storm: high fever, severe tachycardia/AF, delirium, GI dysfunction.
  - Myxoedema coma: hypothermia, bradycardia, hypoventilation, hyponatraemia, hypoglycaemia; treat steroids first if adrenal insufficiency not excluded.
- Pheochromocytoma:
  - Paroxysmal headache, sweating, palpitations, labile/severe hypertension.
- Toxic/metabolic differentials:
  - Pyroglutamic acidosis: high AG metabolic acidosis in elderly/female on chronic paracetamol/sepsis/malnutrition.
  - Toxic alcohols: osmolar gap present.

Arabic (مختصر): اذكر علامات الخطر: صدمة لا تستجيب للسوائل → هيدروكورتيزون؛ صرع/اضطراب وعي بفرط ناقص الصوديوم → سالين مفرط التركيز؛ DKA بدون سكر مرتفع → فكر SGLT2i.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- Immediate bedside/urgent tests:
  - Capillary and serum glucose; VBG/ABG (pH, HCO3); serum electrolytes (Na, K, Cl, HCO3), urea, creatinine, calcium; serum osmolality (measured if possible); urine osmolality and urine Na; blood ketones; random cortisol; ECG.
- Calculations and key rules:
  - Anion gap = Na − (Cl + HCO3). Normal ≈ 8–12 mmol/L.
  - Estimated serum osmolality = 2×Na + urea + glucose (all mmol/L). Osmolar gap = measured − calculated; gap >10–20 suggests toxic alcohols.
  - Winter’s formula for expected pCO2 in metabolic acidosis: pCO2 ≈ 1.5×HCO3 + 8 ±2 (use to detect mixed disorders).
- Syndrome diagnostic cues:
  - DKA (JBDS DKA guidance): ketones positive, pH <7.3 and/or HCO3 <15 mmol/L, plus hyperglycaemia (commonly >11 mmol/L). Anion gap supportive but not required.
  - SIADH: hypotonic serum, urine osmolality inappropriately >100 mOsm/kg, urine Na >30–40 mmol/L, exclude adrenal/thyroid/renal causes.
  - DI: dilute urine despite hyperosmolar plasma; formal water‑deprivation and desmopressin tests in non‑acute settings; in post‑op acute DI treat empirically.
  - Pituitary apoplexy: urgent MRI with contrast (CT may show haemorrhage but MRI more sensitive) [Society for Endocrinology 2010].
- Practical interpretation tips:
  - Serum K in DKA may be misleadingly normal/high; always correct K before insulin if <3.3 mmol/L.
  - In suspected pheochromocytoma crisis, plasma/urine metanephrines confirm diagnosis but do not delay acute stabilisation.

Arabic (مختصر): فحوص فورية: غلوكوز، غازات دموية، إلكتروليتات، كيتونات، أوزمولالية/بولية. حسابات: Anion gap و osmolar gap و Winters' تُفيد التفريق بين الاضطرابات.

4. Emergency management algorithms
(Keep ABCs and monitor ECG throughout; call endocrine/ICU/neurosurgery as indicated.)

Adrenal crisis (suspected/confirmed)
1. ABC, IV access, fluids (0.9% NaCl).
2. Give hydrocortisone 100 mg IV stat. Then either 200 mg/24h as continuous infusion OR 50 mg IV q6h. Do not delay steroids for testing. [Society for Endocrinology 2010]
3. Replace fluids/electrolytes, monitor glucose and K+. Add fludrocortisone later if primary adrenal insufficiency once stable.

Pituitary apoplexy
1. ABC, IV fluids.
2. Give IV hydrocortisone 100 mg immediately, then 50 mg q6h or infusion.
3. Urgent endocrine + neurosurgical review; urgent MRI. Surgical decompression for visual/neurological compromise.

DKA (adult) — initial algorithm (JBDS 2023)
1. ABCs, cardiac monitoring, IV access, bloods (glucose, VBG, electrolytes, ketones).
2. Fluid resuscitation: isotonic crystalloid bolus if hypovolaemic; then guided replacement.
3. Potassium: if K <3.3 mmol/L → replace and HOLD insulin; if 3.3–5.5 → replace per protocol while giving insulin; if >5.5 → withhold K+ and monitor.
4. Insulin: fixed‑rate IV infusion 0.1 U/kg/hr. If glucose fall <3 mmol/L/hr, increase by 1 U/hr. When glucose ≈14 mmol/L, add dextrose and continue insulin until acidosis resolves.
5. Transition: give subcutaneous insulin and overlap before stopping infusion once clinically improving and eating.

Euglycaemic DKA (SGLT2 inhibitor)
- Treat as DKA: fluids + fixed‑rate insulin infusion + dextrose early if glucose <14 mmol/L so insulin can continue. Stop SGLT2 inhibitor. [RCEMLearning]

HHS
- Aggressive IV fluids (restore volume); insulin later at lower doses after initial rehydration; monitor Na and osmolality; high VTE risk — thromboprophylaxis.

Severe hyponatraemia (severe neuro signs)
1. Give small boluses of hypertonic saline (eg 100–150 mL of 3% saline) per local protocol to raise Na by ~4–5 mmol/L to control life‑threatening features.
2. Then slow correction targeting ≈8 mmol/L in 24 h (local protocol dependent). Monitor Na hourly initially.
3. If SIADH: stop offending drugs, fluid restrict, consider demeclocycline/vaptans only with specialist input.

Central DI (acute, post‑op)
- If symptomatic: give desmopressin (IV/SC in acute setting) and replace fluids; monitor Na and urine output hourly; beware delayed hyponatraemia.

Thyroid storm
1. Supportive care, cooling, oxygen, fluids.
2. Beta‑blocker for rate control (propranolol common; esmolol if short‑acting required) unless contraindicated.
3. Give antithyroid drug (PTU preferred in storm for T4→T3 conversion block) then iodine after antithyroid drug.
4. Give glucocorticoids (eg hydrocortisone) and identify precipitant.

Myxoedema coma
- Give IV hydrocortisone 100 mg stat (cover possible adrenal insufficiency), then IV levothyroxine (lower dose if elderly/cardiac disease). Supportive care and treat precipitant.

Pheochromocytoma crisis
- Start alpha blockade (eg phentolamine bolus/infusion or oral/IV selective alpha‑1 blockade); only add beta blockade after adequate alpha blockade. Urgent ICU/specialist input.

Hypercalcaemia (severe/symptomatic)
- IV isotonic saline to restore intravascular volume and promote calciuresis; consider loop diuretics after euvolaemia; IV bisphosphonates for sustained reduction; calcitonin for short‑term effect. Specialist input.

Toxic high anion gap acidosis (pyroglutamic / toxic alcohols)
- Treat underlying cause, supportive care, consider fomepizole/ethanol and dialysis for toxic alcohols. Pyroglutamic: consider stopping paracetamol and treating sepsis/malnutrition contributors; specialist toxicology advice.

Arabic (مختصر): خوارزميات العملية: أزمة أدريال → هيدروكورتيزون 100 mg IV فوري؛ DKA → سوائل، إنسولين ثابت 0.1 U/kg/h، مراقبة بوتاسيوم؛ نقص صوديوم شديد → دفعات صغيرة سلفاتى مركز 3% ثم تصحيح بطيء؛ تجنب إعطاء حاصرات بيتا وحدها في فيئوكروموسايتوما.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
- Key emergency doses (widely used/established):
  - Hydrocortisone in adrenal crisis/pituitary apoplexy/myxoedema cover: 100 mg IV stat → then 200 mg/24 h either infusion or 50 mg IV q6h [Society for Endocrinology 2010].
  - DKA insulin: fixed‑rate infusion 0.1 U/kg/h (JBDS DKA 2023).
  - Potassium thresholds: if K <3.3 mmol/L → replace before insulin; 3.3–5.5 mmol/L → replace while giving insulin; >5.5 mmol/L → withhold replacement and monitor.
  - Severe hyponatraemia: hypertonic saline small boluses (eg 100–150 mL 3% saline) per local protocol to raise Na by 4–5 mmol/L initially.
  - Desmopressin: IV/SC/IN depending on acuity; use IV in acute post‑op DI.
  - Phentolamine bolus often cited (acute pheochromocytoma): specialist/ICU dosing (eg 2–5 mg IV bolus) — verify local guidance.
- Procedures:
  - Urgent MRI pituitary for suspected apoplexy; CT if MRI delayed.
  - ICU admission indications: shock, need for vasoactive support, ventilatory support, severe altered mental state, refractory electrolyte disturbance (e.g., severe DKA/HHS), severe thyroid storm, pheochromocytoma crisis.
  - Endocrine specialty review for all adrenal crisis, pituitary apoplexy, severe hypercalcaemia, persistent DI/SIADH, or complex replacement decisions.
- Disposition & follow‑up:
  - Admit DKA/HHS patients to monitored ward or ICU per severity; arrange diabetes specialist input and clear sick‑day advice.
  - Adrenal crisis survivors: endocrinology follow‑up, education on steroid sick‑day rules, steroid card.
  - Pituitary apoplexy: early endocrine + neurosurgical follow‑up; visual field testing and pituitary function tests when stable.
  - SIADH/hyponatraemia: identify cause, arrange endocrinology/medical follow‑up and consider slow outpatient correction if stable.
  - Pheochromocytoma suspicion: specialist follow‑up for biochemical testing and surgical planning after acute control.
- Practical procedural pitfalls:
  - Do not delay steroids for cortisol testing in unstable patients.
  - Obtain baseline labs (including cortisol, thyroid function) prior to definitive therapy when feasible, but stabilise first.

Arabic (مختصر): جرعات رئيسية: هيدروكورتيزون 100 mg IV فوري ثم 200 mg/24h؛ إنسولين DKA 0.1 U/kg/h؛ دفعات صغيرة من 3% صوديوم لمصابين بنقص صوديوم حاد؛ مراجعة إندكرينولوجى و/أو ICU حسب الحاجة.

6. High‑yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- Do not delay empiric IV hydrocortisone in suspected adrenal crisis or pituitary apoplexy — exam frequently tests this.
- In DKA:
  - Remember fixed‑rate insulin 0.1 U/kg/h (JBDS 2023) and K+ management: if K <3.3 → replace before insulin; do not let insulin precipitate hypokalaemia.
  - Euglycaemic DKA is a trap — normal glucose does not exclude DKA (think SGLT2i).
- In pheochromocytoma: always alpha‑block before beta‑block. Exam scenario trick: starting beta‑block alone precipitates hypertensive crisis.
- Thyroid storm/myxoedema: steroid coverage before giving thyroid hormone if adrenal insufficiency possible.
- Hyponatraemia: correction speed is commonly examined — avoid overcorrection (target ≈8 mmol/L/24 h unless local guideline states otherwise).
- Calculations: practise anion gap, osmolar gap, and Winters’ quickly — often required in written/short answer questions.
- Diagnostic order: remember which tests to do immediately (glucose, VBG, ketones, electrolytes, urine osm, cortisol) vs. specialist tests (plasma metanephrines, pituitary MRI).
- Watch for delayed effects: desmopressin can cause delayed hyponatraemia; correcting Na too fast → osmotic demyelination days later.
- When asked to prioritise: stabilise ABC and correct immediately life‑threatening biochemistry (eg hypoglycaemia, adrenal crisis, severe hyponatraemia with seizures) before definitive investigations.

Arabic (مختصر): حيل للامتحان: اذكر إعطاء الستيرويد فوراً في اشتباه أزمة قشرية؛ إنسولين DKA 0.1 U/kg/h ومراقبة بوتاسيوم؛ لا تتوقف على سكر طبيعي لاستبعاد DKA (فكر SGLT2i).

7. Arabic rapid-review summary
- النقاط الأساسية السريعة:
  - الطوارئ الهرمونية = اضطراب استقلابى + اختلال حجم/إلكتروليتات أو خلل عصبي/قلبي؛ افعل ABC ثم الفحوص الأساسية.
  - أزمة قشرية: هيدروكورتيزون 100 mg IV فوري + سوائل؛ لا تؤجل الستيرويد للفحوص.
  - DKA: سوائل، إنسولين ثابت 0.1 U/kg/h، مراقبة بوتاسيوم؛ في SGLT2i قد يكون السكر طبيعياً — افحص الكيتونات والحموضة.
  - نقص صوديوم حاد مع أعراض عصبية: دفعات صغيرة من 3%؛ ثم تصحيح بطيء (≈8 mmol/L/24h حسب بروتوكول).
  - DI مركزي ما بعد عملية: ديسموبريسين IV/SC + مراقبة صوديوم وكمية البول.
  - Pituitary apoplexy: هيدروكورتيزون فورى + MRI عاجل + جراحة عصبية عند تدهور بصري/عصبى.
  - في فييوكروموسايتوما: احكم ألفا قبل بيتا؛ تفادى إعطاء بيتا بمفرده.
  - احفظ حسابات Anion gap وOsmolar gap وWinter’s formula لتطبيق سريع في الامتحان.

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- Note: original chunk provided concepts rather than numbered question lists. Map of concepts likely to appear in FRCEM coverage:
  - DKA (including euglycaemic DKA, JBDS DKA 2023) — diagnosis & management
  - HHS — differentiation from DKA and initial resuscitation
  - Adrenal crisis and steroid sick‑day rules — immediate management
  - Pituitary apoplexy — recognition, steroids, imaging, neurosurgical indications
  - Thyroid storm and myxoedema coma — initial sequence and treatments
  - SIADH and hyponatraemia — classification, emergency hypertonic therapy, correction limits
  - Central diabetes insipidus (post‑op) — diagnosis & desmopressin use
  - Pheochromocytoma crisis — alpha then beta blockade, acute control
  - Hypercalcaemia — fluids, bisphosphonates, calcitonin
  - Toxic metabolic acidoses: pyroglutamic acidosis, toxic alcohols — AG/osmolar gap & dialysis considerations
  - Interpretation skills: anion gap, osmolar gap, Winter’s formula, ECG changes with electrolytes
- If mapping to exam question banks: these topics commonly span multiple short‑answer and SBAs across endocrine and metabolic sections — ensure practice across DKA/acid‑base, electrolyte emergencies, pituitary emergencies, and thyroid crises.

Arabic (مختصر): خريطة التغطية: DKA/ euDKA، HHS، أزمة قشرية، نزف النخامية، أزمة درقية/كومـا الدرقية، نقص صوديوم/SIADH، DI مركزي، فيوكروموسايتوما، فرط كالسيوم، الحموضات السامة — حسابات Anion/osmolar/Winters.

Sources to verify
- JBDS DKA guideline (2023), Society for Endocrinology guidance (pituitary apoplexy 2010), RCEM / RCEMLearning resources, NICE and ERC acute care guidelines, local hospital endocrine protocols, and toxicology resources (eg. Life in the Fast Lane / LitFL for pyroglutamic). Always check your local trust guideline for exact drug doses and infusion regimens.

(Study material — not patient‑specific advice.)


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 28: ELDERLY CARE
============================================================================================

Chapter 28 — Elderly Care

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Age-related change summary: immunosenescence, reduced fever response, altered physiology (less physiologic reserve), higher prevalence of multimorbidity and polypharmacy → atypical presentations (delirium, falls, reduced oral intake), higher baseline prevalence of asymptomatic bacteriuria.  
- Catheter-related: chronic indwelling catheters increase bacteriuria risk, biofilm formation, and specific phenomena (purple urine bag syndrome = bacterial tryptophan metabolism producing indigo/indirubin pigments). [BMJ Case Rep 2018]  
- Pharmacology/haemodynamics: common offending drugs for orthostatic hypotension include alpha‑1 blockers (e.g., tamsulosin), other antihypertensives, diuretics; autonomic dysfunction (eg, diabetic neuropathy) and volume depletion magnify risk.  
- Capacity: decision-specific; dementia ≠ automatic lack of capacity. Always assess capacity for the specific treatment/consent decision at hand.

Arabic: 
موجز فسيولوجي: التقدم في العمر يقلّل الدفاعات المناعية ويغيّر عتبات الأعراض؛ الأمراض المتعددة والأدوية المتعدية تؤدي لعرض غير نمطي مثل الارتباك والسقوط. القثاطر المزمنة تزيد البكتيريّة وتسبب ظاهرة كيس البول البنفسجي (استقلاب التريبتوفان). تقييم الأهلية قرار‑محدّد—الخرف لا يعني فقدان الأهلية تلقائياً.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Atypical infection presentation: delirium, new/acute decline in mobility/function, anorexia, falls, in absence of fever or urinary symptoms. Differential: asymptomatic bacteriuria, medication effect, constipation, dehydration, sepsis from non‑urinary source, heart failure, pneumonia, metabolic disturbance.  
- PUBS: purple discoloration of catheter/bag in long‑term catheter users suggests heavy bacteriuria; treat based on clinical infection, not colour alone. [BMJ Case Rep 2018]  
- Orthostatic symptoms: consider medications, volume depletion, cardiac arrhythmia, anaemia, autonomic failure. Measure orthostatic BPs (see diagnostic section). Diagnostic thresholds: fall ≥20 mmHg systolic or ≥10 mmHg diastolic on standing.  
- Red flags requiring urgent action: signs of sepsis (hypotension, tachycardia, high lactate), hypoxia, altered level of consciousness, inability to maintain oral meds/fluids, perineal/urosepsis signs, obstructive uropathy.

Arabic:
التعرّف والتمييز: لدى المسنين قد تكون العدوى بدون حمّى؛ توقع الارتباك والسقوط كعلامات. كيس البول البنفسجي مؤشر لبكتيريّة عالية ولكن القرار العلاجي يعتمد على الأعراض النظامية. هبوط الضغط الانتصابي يُشتبه به بتراجع ≥20/≥10 ممHg. علامات الخطر: صدمة إنتانية، نقص أكسجين، تغيّر الوعي، عدم القدرة على تناول السوائل/الأدوية.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- Initial steps: ABC, sepsis screen if unwell, focused history (catheter, recent antibiotics, functional baseline, medications), medication review.  
- Urinary testing:
  - Do bedside urine dipstick (LE/nitrite) but interpret cautiously — dipstick alone is unreliable in older adults; false positives/positives from asymptomatic bacteriuria common (verify local guidance, age threshold often ≥65).  
  - Send urine culture (catheter specimen if catheter present) when clinical infection suspected; do not treat culture‑positive but asymptomatic patients (exceptions: pregnancy, pre‑urological procedure).  
- Blood tests: CBC, CRP, renal function, electrolytes; blood cultures if febrile/septic; serum lactate if sepsis suspected.  
- Imaging: renal tract ultrasound/CT only for complicated/recurrent infections or suspected obstruction/abscess.  
- Orthostatic BP measurement technique: measure supine after 5 minutes, then standing measurements at 1 and 3 minutes. Record symptoms with drops.  
- PUBS: obtain catheter urine culture, change catheter/bag, examine for systemic infection before prescribing antibiotics. [BMJ Case Rep 2018]

Arabic:
النهج التشخيصي: ابدأ بتقييم ABC وشاشة الإنتان، راجع الأدوية وحالة القثطار. شرائط البول مفيدة مبدئياً ولكن غير كافية عند كبار السن؛ أرسل مزرعة بول عند الشك السريري. تحاليل دمّية ومزرعة دم إذا اشتبهت بإنتان نظامي. التصوير مخصص للمضاعفات أو الانسداد. قياس ضغط الدم الانتصابي بعد الاستلقاء 5 دقائق ثم عند 1 و3 دقائق. عند الكيس البنفسجي خذ مزرعة وغيّر القثطار وقيّم وجود أعراض نظامية.

4. Emergency management algorithms
- Algorithm A — Suspected UTI in elderly (compact):
  1. ABC + sepsis screen (vitals, lactate if available).  
  2. If sepsis criteria or haemodynamic instability: give immediate resuscitation (oxygen as needed, IV access, fluids if hypovolaemic), blood cultures, lactate, empiric IV antibiotics promptly per local protocol; change/remove infected catheter if source. Admit to appropriate monitored setting.  
  3. If not septic but symptomatic (dysuria, fever, new urinary frequency, suprapubic pain, or atypical features with clinical suspicion): send urine culture, consider oral antibiotics based on severity/local guidance; arrange follow‑up and review culture results.  
  4. If asymptomatic bacteriuria: do not treat unless clear indication (pregnancy/urology).  
- Algorithm B — Capacity assessment for treatment decisions:
  1. Presume capacity. Explain decision, options, risks, benefits.  
  2. Assess understanding, retention, weighing, and ability to communicate decision.  
  3. If capacity present, follow patient’s informed decision. If lacking, use best‑interests process (advance directives, nominated proxy, multidisciplinary input). Document thoroughly.  
- Algorithm C — Orthostatic dizziness/falls:
  1. Assess ABCs, check injuries. Measure supine (after 5 min) then standing BP at 1 and 3 min.  
  2. If diagnostic drop: review and stop/hold offending meds (alpha‑blocker, diuretic, antihypertensive), assess for hypovolaemia and give fluids if appropriate, institute fall precautions, consider senior/geriatric/neurology referral for recurrent cases. Specialist agents (midodrine/fludrocortisone) only after specialist review.

Arabic:
خوارزميات طارئة:  
A — عدوى المسالك: افحص الإنتان أولاً؛ إذا صدق الإنتان أعطِ resuscitation، مزرعات، لا تتأخر في المضاد الحيوي وريدي وغير القثطار إذا مصدر العدوى. في الحالات الخفيفة أرسل مزرعة واعتبر مضاداً فموياً حسب الدليل المحلي. لا تعالج بكتيريا البول بدون أعراض.  
B — تقييم الأهلية: افترض الأهلية ثم قيّم الفهم والقدرة واتبع رغبة المريض أو مبدأ المصلحة الفضلى إذا فقد الأهلية.  
C — دوار النهوض: قِس الضغط بعد الاستلقاء و1 و3 دقائق؛ أوقف الأدوية المسببة، عوّض الحجم إذا لازِم، اتخذ إجراءات منع السقوط وأحال عند التكرار.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
- Drugs:
  - Empiric antimicrobials: follow local microbiology guidance; obtain cultures before antibiotics when possible in non‑critical patients. Avoid treating asymptomatic bacteriuria.  
  - Medications to withhold/stop if orthostatic hypotension suspected: alpha‑1 blockers (tamsulosin), diuretics, excessive antihypertensives — individualise.  
  - Specialist pharmacotherapy for chronic orthostatic hypotension (midodrine, fludrocortisone) only after specialist review.  
- Procedures:
  - Obtain urine culture (catheter sample if present), blood cultures if sepsis.  
  - Change or remove indwelling catheter if suspected catheter‑related infection or for PUBS management (replace catheter and bag). [BMJ Case Rep 2018]  
  - Imaging only when indicated (obstruction, recurrent/complicated infection, suspected abscess).  
- Disposition:
  - Admit: any sepsis/haemodynamic instability, hypoxia, altered consciousness, inability to maintain oral intake/meds, complicated UTI, obstructive uropathy, recurrent falls with risk.  
  - Consider ED discharge with safety net and oral antibiotics when stable, tolerating PO, able to self‑care or with reliable carers, and follow‑up arranged.  
- Follow‑up and prevention:
  - Review urine culture results and de‑escalate/stop antibiotics accordingly.  
  - Review and rationalise medications (polypharmacy review). Consider fall‑prevention measures, post‑falls assessment, community/GP/geriatric follow‑up, catheter care education, and treatment plans for recurrent UTIs.  
  - Document capacity assessment, best‑interests decisions, and escalation plan.

Arabic:
الأدوية والإجراءات والتصرّف والمتابعة:  
الأدوية: ابدأ مضاداً حيوياً وفقاً لدليل الأحياء الدقيقة المحلي بعد أخذ المزروعات؛ لا تعالج بكتيريا البول بدون أعراض. إذا شُكّ في هبوط الانتصاب أوقف مثبطات ألفا‑1 ومدرات البول ومخفضات الضغط المناسبة مؤقتاً. الأدوية المتخصصة تُستخدم بتوجيه أخصائي.  
الإجراءات: مزرعة بول/دم، تغيير القثطار عند الاشتباه بعدوى أو الكيس البنفسجي، تصوير للمضاعفات فقط.  
التصرّف: دخول المستشفى عند الإنتان، عدم الاستقرار أو عدم القدرة على الرعاية بالمنازل؛ تصريف خارجي عند الاستقرار مع تعليم ومتابعة. المتابعة: تعديل الأدوية، مراجعة ثقافات البول، خطط منع السقوط، إحالة لطب الشيوخ والمجتمع.

6. High-yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- Do not equate dementia with incapacity — exam vignettes often try to trap with that assumption. Always answer using the decision‑specific capacity framework.  
- Do not treat positive dipstick or urine culture alone in elderly patients — look for clinical signs of infection. Treating asymptomatic bacteriuria is a common error. (Verify local age thresholds/guidance.)  
- PUBS: the purple bag is a clue to bacteriuria but not an automatic indication for antibiotics — change catheter/bag and assess systemic features first. [BMJ Case Rep 2018]  
- Orthostatic BP measurement: measure at 1 and 3 minutes — immediate only readings can miss delayed orthostatic drops.  
- Always ask “is there a catheter?” early in the assessment; review medications as a frequent reversible cause of symptoms.  
- Documentation: clear record of capacity assessment, rationale for treating or not treating bacteriuria, and decision to change or leave catheter are scored in viva/OSCEs.

Arabic:
حيل الامتحان والمصائد:  
لا تفترض فقدان الأهلية بسبب الخرف. لا تعالج نتائج البول الموجبة دون دليل سريري. الكيس البنفسجي دليل فقط—غيّر القثطار وقَيّم النظامي قبل العلاج بالمضاد الحيوي. قِس الضغط عند 1 و3 دقائق لتفادي الأخطاء. اسأل مبكراً عن وجود قثطار وراجع الأدوية. وثّق قرارات الأهلية والعلاج جيداً.

7. Arabic rapid-review summary
- المسنّون يعرضون عرضاً غير نمطي للعدوى (ارتباك، سقوط، قلة تناول).  
- شرائط البول وحدها غير كافية عند كبار السن؛ مزرعة بول ضرورية إذا تُعالج العدوى.  
- لا تعالج بكتيريا البول بدون أعراض ما لم توجد موانع/استثناءات واضحة (حمل، تدخلات بولية).  
- كيس البول البنفسجي يدل غالباً على بكتيريويا في القثطار — غيّر القثطار وقيّم وجود أعراض نظامية قبل العلاج بالمضادات الحيوية. [BMJ Case Rep 2018]  
- هبوط الضغط الانتصابي يُقاس بعد الاستلقاء 5 دقائق ثم عند 1 و3 دقائق؛ راجع الأدوية وغيّرها إذا لزم.  
- قيّم وتوثّق القدرة لقرار العلاج بشكل محدّد لكل قرار.

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- Concepts covered (source Q‑numbers not provided):  
  - Atypical infection presentations in the elderly (delirium, falls).  
  - Asymptomatic bacteriuria vs UTI; limitations of urine dipstick in ≥65.  
  - Catheter‑associated issues including purple urine bag syndrome (PUBS) — diagnosis and management. [BMJ Case Rep 2018]  
  - Orthostatic hypotension: measurement, causes (drugs, volume, autonomic), immediate ED management.  
  - Capacity assessment principles—decision‑specific model.  
  - Sepsis recognition and initial resuscitation/antibiotic principles in elderly patients.  
  - Practical disposition decisions (admit vs discharge, follow‑up).  
- Note: no original question numbering was supplied with the chunk notes; map therefore lists covered concepts rather than Q‑ranges.

Sources to verify
- RCEM guidelines, ERC/European Resuscitation Council, NICE guidance (urinary tract infection and sepsis), Surviving Sepsis Campaign, IDSA guidance on asymptomatic bacteriuria, BMJ Case Reports 2018 (purple urine bag syndrome), local microbiology and trust antibiotic policies.


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 29: EAR, NOSE AND THROAT
============================================================================================

Chapter 29 — Ear, Nose and Throat (ENT)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Core priorities: secure airway, control life‑threatening haemorrhage, treat invasive infection, prevent cartilage necrosis (auricle, septum), preserve hearing. ENT emergencies often threaten airway, cause major bleeding, or progress to sepsis/osteomyelitis.
- Common pathophysiologic clusters:
  - External ear: otitis externa → local cellulitis; malignant (necrotizing) otitis externa → Pseudomonas invasion of soft tissue → skull‑base osteomyelitis in diabetics/immunocompromised.
  - Middle/inner ear and hearing: TM perforation allows middle‑ear infection; cochlear injury causes sensorineural loss. SSNHL = rapid cochlear/nerve dysfunction.
  - Vestibular: canalithiasis (BPPV) = brief position‑triggered vertigo; vestibular neuritis = prolonged peripheral vertigo without cochlear signs; central causes (stroke, tumour) produce atypical nystagmus/focal signs.
  - Nasal and oropharyngeal bleeding: anterior (Kiesselbach) vs posterior (sphenopalatine/arterial) sources; post‑operative bleeds risk major haemorrhage.
  - Soft‑tissue/space infections: peritonsillar, parapharyngeal, retropharyngeal, Ludwig’s — risk airway compromise and septic thromboembolism (Lemierre’s).
  - Traumatic/cartilage injuries: auricular or septal haematomas disrupt perichondrial blood supply → cartilage necrosis if not drained.

شرح عربي موجز:
- الأولوية: تأمين مجرى الهواء، إيقاف النزف الخطير، معالجة العدوى الغازية، ومنع نخر الغضروف والحفاظ على السمع.
- الفشل في تصريف الهيماتوما أو تأخير علاج التهابات الأذن الخارجية في مرضى السكر قد يؤديان إلى مضاعفات عظمية خطيرة.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Ear:
  - Otitis externa: ear pain, pruritus, otorrhea after swimming; granulation tissue + severe pain in diabetic → malignant otitis externa (Pseudomonas) → red flag: cranial nerve palsy, severe refractory pain.
  - Sudden sensorineural hearing loss (SSNHL): rapid unilateral loss (≥30 dB across ≥3 contiguous frequencies within 72 h) — ENT emergency.
  - Mastoiditis: post‑otitis pain, swelling behind auricle, displaced pinna, systemic signs.
- Vertigo syndromes:
  - BPPV: brief (<1 min) position‑triggered vertigo, latency then crescendo/decrescendo nystagmus.
  - Vestibular neuritis: single prolonged attack (days), severe vertigo, horizontal unidirectional nystagmus, no hearing loss.
  - Ménière’s: episodic vertigo + fluctuating hearing loss, tinnitus, aural fullness.
  - Central (posterior circulation stroke): acute vertigo with focal neuro signs, direction‑changing or vertical nystagmus, normal head impulse; red flag: vascular risk factors.
- Throat/neck:
  - Peritonsillar abscess: trismus, muffled “hot‑potato” voice, uvula deviation away from lesion.
  - Lemierre’s syndrome: preceding oropharyngeal infection, sepsis, IJV tenderness/thrombosis, pulmonary septic emboli.
  - Deep neck space infection or post‑op neck haematoma: progressive airway compromise → immediate escalation.
- Nose:
  - Anterior epistaxis: bright brisk anterior bleed from Little’s area; posterior bleed: sudden profuse hemorrhage, older/comorbid patients.
- Tracheostomy/tracheo‑innominate fistula: sentinel bleeding from stoma → massive hemorrhage risk; red flag: brisk arterial haemorrhage, haemodynamic collapse.

شرح عربي موجز:
- العلامات الحمراء: شَلَل عصبي محيطي أو مركزي مع ألم أذني شديد، نقص سمع مفاجئ، تدهور تنفّسي أو نزيف عنيف من فغر القصبة أو الأنف، أو هيماتوما غضروفي لم يُصرف.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- General ED priorities: ABC, haemodynamic resuscitation, analgesia/antiemetics for vertigo, early senior ENT/anesthesia input for airway/major bleeds.
- Ear:
  - Otoscopy: canal debris, TM status (intact vs perforated), granulation tissue. Do not irrigate if TM perforation suspected.
  - Ear swab for culture if purulent or malignant OE suspected; check glucose/HbA1c in diabetics; ESR/CRP for osteomyelitis workup.
  - Imaging: CT temporal bones for mastoiditis/osseous erosion; MRI skull base for suspected skull‑base osteomyelitis.
- Vertigo:
  - Dix–Hallpike for positional vertigo (BPPV). If continuous vestibular syndrome: HINTS (Head‑Impulse, Nystagmus direction, Test‑of‑Skew) — use only in ongoing vertigo with nystagmus and by trained clinicians; HINTS suggesting central cause = normal head impulse + direction‑changing nystagmus or skew deviation. If HINTS suggests central or examiner uncertain → MRI DWI.
- Throat/neck:
  - Peritonsillar abscess: clinical diagnosis; consider needle aspiration; ultrasound or CT neck with contrast if deep space infection suspected.
  - Lemierre’s: blood cultures, CT neck/chest for IJV thrombosis and septic emboli.
- Nose:
  - Anterior rhinoscopy to localise bleeding; posterior bleeds suspected when bleed is heavy and not controlled with anterior measures — consider nasal endoscopy or imaging.
- Tracheostomy bleed:
  - Bedside recognition: fresh arterial bleeding from stoma; do not remove a tracheostomy tube casually — follow bleeding algorithm (cuff over‑inflation, digital compression).

شرح عربي موجز:
- التشخيص يعتمد على فحص الأذن بالمصباح، اختبار Dix–Hallpike للدوار الموضعي، وHINTS للدوار المستمر (يُجرى بواسطة فاحص متمرس). استخدم CT/MRI لتقييم التعقيدات العظمية أو الخِرّاجات العميقة.

4. Emergency management algorithms
- Epistaxis — anterior first aid → escalation:
  1. Sit upright, lean forward, pinch soft nasal ala over Little’s area for 10–15 min continuous.
  2. If persistent: topical vasoconstrictor (oxymetazoline/phenylephrine) ± topical anaesthetic; identify visible source → chemical (silver nitrate) or electrocautery.
  3. If still bleeding: anterior packing (absorbable or non‑absorbable) — document pack type/time; duration typically 24–48 h.
  4. If fails or posterior suspicion: posterior packing (balloon/Foley or commercial device) and urgent ENT/IR for endoscopic ligation or embolisation.
  5. Reverse anticoagulation if indicated; consider TXA as adjunct per local protocol.
- Tracheostomy / tracheo‑innominate fistula (major haemorrhage):
  1. Immediate: over‑inflate tracheostomy cuff to tamponade.
  2. If bleeding persists: insert finger into stoma, press posteriorly to compress brachiocephalic artery against sternum.
  3. Activate major haemorrhage/team, call ENT/vascular surgery/anesthesia, prepare for theatre or OR airway.
  4. Do not remove tracheostomy unless required for intubation; if tube displaced, consider oral intubation or tracheostomy revision by expert.
  (Algorithm derived from RCEM learning modules) [RCEM].
- Peritonsillar abscess:
  1. Airway assessment and analgesia.
  2. Needle aspiration or incision & drainage (ENT) ± immediate antibiotics covering streptococci and anaerobes.
  3. Single dose dexamethasone reduces symptoms and may ease airway concerns [BMJ j4090].
- Auricular/septal haematoma:
  1. Immediate drainage (aspiration or I+D) + compression dressing to prevent cartilage necrosis (cauliflower ear / saddle deformity).
  2. Prophylactic antibiotics covering skin flora; urgent ENT/plastic review for packing/suture.
- Otitis externa vs malignant OE:
  - Otitis externa: topical therapy, aural toilet.
  - Malignant OE: admit, ear swab, IV antipseudomonal antibiotics, ENT/ID input, image to assess skull‑base.
- Ear foreign body / live insect:
  1. If live insect: instil topical lidocaine 2% to immobilize/kill then remove with suction/forceps.
  2. Do not irrigate if suspect TM perforation or hygroscopic objects; ENT review for difficult removals/child not cooperative.
- BPPV:
  1. Diagnose with Dix–Hallpike.
  2. Treat with Epley canalith repositioning manoeuvre on affected side; repeat/refer to physiotherapy if recurrent.
- SSNHL:
  1. Urgent audiometry and ENT referral.
  2. Start systemic corticosteroids early (ideally within days; many ENT guidelines within 14 days) or intratympanic steroids per ENT advice.
- Post‑tonsillectomy haemorrhage:
  1. ABCs, large‑bore IV access, suction to visualise, topical vasoconstrictors and direct pressure.
  2. Give TXA as adjunctive haemostatic therapy while arranging definitive ENT operative control (paediatric TXA 15 mg/kg IV bolus cited in practice; adults frequently 1 g IV) — verify local protocols.
  3. Urgent ENT/anaesthesia for theatre control if ongoing or recurrent bleeding.
- Deep neck infections / Lemierre’s:
  1. Secure airway early if threatened.
  2. Empiric broad IV antibiotics covering anaerobes (metronidazole) and aerobes (e.g., ceftriaxone) pending cultures; urgent imaging (CT neck/chest) and specialist input.
  3. Anticoagulation for IJV thrombosis is specialist‑dependent.

شرح عربي موجز:
- إجراءات عاجلة واضحة: نزيف أنفي → ضغط أمامي أولاً ثم احتراق/حشو/حشْو خلفي أو تدخل إشعاعي؛ نزيف فغر القصبة → نفخ كَفّة الأنبوب ثم ضغط إصبعي داخل الفتحة والإنعاش الجراحي السريع.
- نزيف ما بعد استئصال اللوزتين = إنعاش + استدعاء ENT فورياً؛ يمكن استخدام التراكсізاميك أسيد كمعاون وفق البروتوكول المحلي.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
- Key drug cautions and choices:
  - Avoid topical aminoglycoside drops (e.g., neomycin) in TM perforation — ototoxicity risk. Use topical fluoroquinolone (ciprofloxacin otic) for infected perforations.
  - Malignant otitis externa: systemic antipseudomonal antibiotics (IV) per local formulary; prolonged courses often required.
  - SSNHL: systemic corticosteroids (dose per ENT protocol); intratympanic steroids as specialist option.
  - TXA: adjunct in post‑tonsillectomy bleeding — paediatric bolus commonly 15 mg/kg IV over 10 min; adults often 1 g IV — confirm local dosing.
  - Analgesia and antiemetics for vertigo (prochlorperazine/ondansetron; short‑term vestibular sedatives only when necessary).
- Procedures commonly required in ED:
  - Anterior nasal cautery (silver nitrate) for visible anterior epistaxis.
  - Nasal packing (anterior then posterior balloon) — document type/time, provide antibiotics if local policy, and plan removal.
  - Auricular/septal haematoma drainage with compression dressing and ENT follow‑up.
  - Needle aspiration or I&D for peritonsillar abscess; consider steroids to reduce swelling/pain.
  - Epley manoeuvre for posterior canal BPPV.
  - Tracheostomy stoma management: cuff over‑inflation, digital tamponade, urgent specialist transfer.
  - Removal of ear foreign body (lidocaine for live insects; suction/curette under visualization).
- Disposition and follow‑up:
  - Admit: malignant OE, post‑tonsillectomy major bleed, uncontrolled epistaxis/posterior bleed, mastoiditis with complications, tracheostomy bleeding, deep neck space infections, SSNHL awaiting ENT review when immediate treatment required.
  - Discharge with ENT/GP follow‑up: uncomplicated otitis externa (with appropriate drops), resolved BPPV after manoeuvre with outpatient physio, controlled anterior epistaxis after pack removal and ENT review.
  - Document instructions: nasal pack removal time, ear‑drop restrictions (water precautions with TM perforation), return precautions for airway compromise/fever/worsening hearing.

شرح عربي موجز:
- تجنّب الأمينوغليكوزيدات الموضعية مع ثقب غشاء الطبل؛ استخدم سيبروفلوكساسين موضعياً للمثقب المصاب.
- إجراءات شائعة: تصريف هيماتومات الغضروف، تفريغ خراج حول اللوزة، مناورة Epley للدوار البوطي.
- اتجاه النقل: الحالات الغازية أو النزيف غير المسيطر عليه أو المهددة للمجرى الهوائي تحتاج قبولًا بالمستشفى وتحويل ENT/جراحة/تخدير عاجل.

6. High‑yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
- FRCEM tricks:
  - Peritonsillar abscess: uvula deviates away from affected side; presence of trismus points to quinsy rather than simple tonsillitis.
  - Epistaxis first aid: sit up, lean forward, pinch soft nasal ala (not the bony bridge), hold continuously 10–15 min.
  - BPPV vs central positional vertigo: BPPV has latency, fatigable and crescendo/decrescendo nystagmus; central is immediate, non‑fatigable, may be vertical or direction‑changing.
  - HINTS (in the right context) can outperform early MRI for posterior circulation stroke detection in continuous vestibular syndrome — but only if examiner is experienced.
  - SSNHL definition: ≥30 dB loss over ≥3 contiguous frequencies within 72 h → treat as ENT emergency.
  - Ototoxic drops: never use aminoglycoside drops if TM integrity uncertain — examiners love this pitfall.
  - Auricular/septal haematomas require drainage; delayed treatment causes permanent deformity — an easy viva point.
  - Tracheostomy bleed: over‑inflate cuff then digital compression of brachiocephalic artery — a high‑yield emergency mnemonic.
- Common pitfalls:
  - Misinterpreting head tilt/back‑tilt in epistaxis — do not tilt head back.
  - Over‑reliance on Centor/FeverPAIN scores instead of assessing airway risk in throat infections.
  - Using irrigation for ear foreign bodies when TM perforation possible or object is hygroscopic.
  - Using HINTS without ongoing spontaneous nystagmus or adequate examiner training — yields false reassurance.
  - Delaying ENT for suspected malignant otitis externa in diabetics with severe pain or cranial nerve signs.

شرح عربي موجز:
- نصائح الاختبار: الشرط التعريفي لفقدان السمع المفاجئ، تجنّب الأمينوغليكوزيدات مع ثقب الطبل، تذكّر مناورة Epley للـ BPPV وقيّم HINTS فقط إذا كنت متمرساً وفي حالة دوار مستمر.

7. Arabic rapid‑review summary (ملخص عربي سريع)
- الأولويات: تأمين مجرى الهواء، إيقاف النزف الكبير، كشف ومعالجة العدوى الغازية، تفريغ هيماتوما غضروفية فوراً، حفظ السمع.
- نزيف الأنف الأمامي: ضغط للجزء الطري للأذن 10–15 دقيقة، كي موضعي إن ظهر مصدر، حشو أمامي 24–48 ساعة، حشو خلفي أو استدعاء ENT إذا فشل.
- ثقب غشاء الطبل المصاب: لا أمينوغليكوزيدات؛ استخدم قطرات فلوروكينولون موضعية إذا مُصاب.
- دوار: Dix–Hallpike → Epley (BPPV). دوار مستمر → HINTS (بيد خبير)؛ أي إشارة مركزية → تصوير دماغي.
- خراج حول اللوزة: trismus + uvula مبتعدة → سحب بالإبرة أو شق وتصريف + مضاد حيوي ± ستيرويد جرعة واحدة.
- نزيف فغر القصبة الكبير: نفخ كَفّة الأنبوبيّة ثم ضغط إصبعي داخل الفتحة على الشريان، استدعاء جراحي فوري.
- حالات تتطلب دخول مستشفى: OE الخبيث، mastoiditis مع مضاعفات، نزف خلفي أو مستمر، مشکلات ما بعد استئصال اللوزتين، SSNHL إن يحتاج علاج فوري.

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- ENT concepts consolidated from supplied notes (no explicit Q‑numbers given):
  - Epistaxis: anterior vs posterior management, packing, cautery, TXA adjuncts.
  - Otitis externa and malignant OE (Pseudomonas) diagnosis and management.
  - Ear foreign bodies and live insects removal techniques.
  - Tympanic membrane perforation: safe topical choices and water precautions.
  - Auricular and nasal septal haematomas: drainage and prevention of cartilage necrosis.
  - Peritonsillar abscess (quinsy): recognition, aspiration/I&D, antibiotics, steroid adjunct.
  - Lemierre’s syndrome: recognition, imaging, empiric antibiotics.
  - Post‑tonsillectomy haemorrhage: resuscitation, use of TXA as adjunct, operative control.
  - Tracheostomy bleeding / tracheo‑innominate fistula: immediate bedside steps and escalation.
  - Vertigo syndromes: BPPV (Dix–Hallpike/Epley), vestibular neuritis, Ménière’s, central vertigo and HINTS.
  - Sudden sensorineural hearing loss (SSNHL): diagnostic thresholds, urgent ENT/audiology referral, steroid therapy.
  - Mastoiditis and deep neck space infections: imaging, indications for surgical intervention.
  - Exam technique pitfalls: HINTS limitations, ear drop ototoxicity, epistaxis first aid errors.

Sources to verify: RCEM learning modules, ENT‑UK guidance, BMJ review (dexamethasone in tonsillitis j4090), NUEM discussions on HINTS, local ENT guidelines, NICE and ERC recommendations where applicable.


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 30: DERMATOLOGY
============================================================================================

Chapter 30 — Dermatology (final)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Organise rash assessment by morphology, distribution, timing and systemic features:
  - Morphology: vesiculobullous, maculopapular/morbilliform, pustular, targetoid, ulcerative/necrotic.
  - Distribution: localized vs widespread; mucosal involvement always significant.
  - Timing: acute (hours–days), delayed (days–weeks) — timing helps distinguish AGEP (rapid), SJS/TEN (1–3 weeks), DRESS (2–6 weeks), drug exanthem (days–weeks).
  - Systemic features: fever, lymphadenopathy, organ dysfunction indicate systemic disease/SCAR.
- Pathophysiology highlights:
  - SCARs (SJS/TEN/DRESS/AGEP) are drug‑triggered immune reactions; epidermal necrosis in SJS/TEN from keratinocyte apoptosis.
  - Autoimmune blistering: intraepidermal acantholysis (pemphigus vulgaris) vs subepidermal cleavage (bullous pemphigoid).
  - Infectious dermatoses have characteristic patterns (erythema migrans, erythema multiforme, impetigo, eczema herpeticum).
  - Pyoderma gangrenosum is neutrophilic dermatosis with pathergy; debridement worsens lesions.
(References: NICE CKS; DermNetNZ)

ملخص بالعربية
- صنف الطفح حسب الشكل، التوزيع والوقت؛ وجود مخاطيات أو علامات جهازية يدل على حالة خطرة.  
- حالات فرط التحسس الجلدية الشديدة غالباً دوائية؛ تختلف الآليات بين انفصال البشرة والطفح الحبيبي والآفات النخريّة.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Red flags (immediate attention):
  - Any mucosal erosions/ulceration, progressive epidermal detachment, haemodynamic instability, airway compromise, ocular involvement, high fever, organ failure signs.
- Key recognition cues and DDX:
  - Target lesions ± limited mucosa → erythema multiforme (HSV/Mycoplasma trigger).
  - Rapid epidermal detachment ± mucosa after drug → SJS/TEN (SJS <10% BSA; overlap 10–30%; TEN >30%).
  - Widespread sterile pustules within 48 h of drug → AGEP (neutrophilia).
  - Fever + facial oedema + rash 2–6 weeks after drug with eosinophilia/organ involvement → DRESS.
  - Tense blisters in elderly → bullous pemphigoid; flaccid blisters with mucosal erosions → pemphigus vulgaris (Nikolsky +).
  - Painful undermined ulcer with violaceous border → pyoderma gangrenosum (IBD/paraneoplastic associations).
  - Clustered linear pruritic papules after sleep → bed‑bug/arthropod bites.
  - Honey‑coloured crusts on face/children → impetigo (Strep/Staph) [NICE CKS].
  - Expanding erythematous annulus with central clearing → tinea corporis (KOH helpful).
  - Eczema herpeticum: disseminated vesiculopustules/erosions on atopic skin + systemic signs → treat with antivirals.
  - Erythema migrans after tick bite → early Lyme (doxycycline) [NICE CKS].
- Pitfalls that mandate caution:
  - Mistaking EM for early SJS; check mucosa and detachment %.  
  - Surgical debridement of suspected PG → pathergy and worse outcome.  
  - Assuming morbilliform rash is benign without looking for systemic features (SCAR risk).

الشرح بالعربية
- أي مشاركة مخاطية أو انفصال جلدي تقدّمية أو علامات جهازية هي علامات حمراء؛ تمييز الأشكال (هدفية، فقاعية، قيحية) يوجّه التشخيص الأولي.  
- احذر استئصال قرحات pyoderma gangrenosum وخلط EM مع المراحل المبكرة من SJS.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- Immediate priorities: ABCs (airway risk from mucosal disease), haemodynamic stabilisation, estimate BSA (palm ≈ 1%).
- Focused history: recent/new drugs (timing), infections, travel, insect exposure, prior dermatoses, immune status.
- Targeted exam: mucosae (oral, ocular, genital), Nikolsky sign, exact morphology, distribution, lymphadenopathy.
- Bedside tests:
  - KOH prep for fungal hyphae.
  - Bacterial swab/culture if purulent/impetiginised.
  - Tzanck smear may show multinucleated giant cells (not specific).
  - Consider skin biopsy for histology/immunofluorescence in bullous diseases or unclear SCAR.
- Baseline labs when systemic features: CBC (neutrophilia/eosinophilia), creatinine/urea, electrolytes, LFTs, glucose, blood cultures if febrile.
- Use SCORTEN (day 1) to prognosticate SJS/TEN: age >40, malignancy, HR >120, BSA detached >10%, urea >10 mmol/L, glucose >14 mmol/L, bicarbonate <20 mmol/L.
- Early specialty input: dermatology ± burns/ICU, ophthalmology (ocular mucosal disease), infectious disease, rheumatology as indicated.

الشرح بالعربية
- أقِم ABCs ثم قدّر مساحة الإصابة (كف اليد ≈ 1%).  
- فحوصات بسيطة: KOH، مسحات بكتيرية، خزعة جلدية عند الحاجة.  
- استخدم SCORTEN لتقدير خطر الوفاة في SJS/TEN واطلب استشارة مبكرة.

4. Emergency management algorithms
A. SJS/TEN (stepwise)
1. Immediate: stop all possible causative drugs.
2. ABCs: monitor airway (early ENT/anaesthesia if mucosal airway risk), oxygen, IV access.
3. Assess BSA (palm ≈ 1%):
   - SJS <10% → consider admission; SJS/TEN overlap 10–30% → high‑dependency; TEN >30% → burns unit/ICU.
4. Resuscitation: IV fluids, correct electrolytes, monitor urine output; treat as burn patient for fluid/electrolyte/infection risk.
5. Wound care: sterile, non‑adhesive dressings; minimal debridement; temperature control and analgesia.
6. Complications: sepsis prevention, nutrition, thromboprophylaxis as appropriate.
7. Ancillary: ophthalmology, dermatologist, burn/ICU input.
8. Disease‑modifying therapy: IVIG, systemic corticosteroids, cyclosporine used variably — consult local protocol/specialist (evidence mixed).
(Use SCORTEN for prognosis)

B. Suspected SCARs (AGEP/DRESS/drug exanthem)
- Stop offending drug. Supportive care: fluids, antipyretics, topical emollients/steroids for pruritus.
- AGEP: usually self‑limited; monitor CBC (neutrophilia).
- DRESS: systemic steroids often required; monitor LFTs, renal function, blood counts; admit if organ involvement.

C. Eczema herpeticum
- Start systemic antiviral promptly:
  - Mild/moderate: oral acyclovir (per local guidance).
  - Severe/disseminated/immunocompromised: IV acyclovir 10 mg/kg IV q8h (adjust for renal function); admit and obtain dermatology/ID/ophthalmology input.

D. Impetigo
- Localised: topical hydrogen peroxide 1% cream first‑line (NICE CKS), or topical fusidic acid/mupirocin per local policy.
- Extensive/systemic signs: oral antibiotics (cover Strep/Staph) and consider admission.

E. Lyme disease (erythema migrans)
- Doxycycline 100 mg PO twice daily in adults (duration per local guideline) [NICE CKS]; pregnancy/children: alternatives.

F. Bullous diseases and pyoderma gangrenosum
- Analgesia, wound care, urgent dermatology. Avoid debridement in PG. Systemic immunosuppression under specialist guidance (e.g., steroids, cyclosporine).

خلاصة بالعربية للخطوات الحرجة
- SJS/TEN: أوقف الأدوية فوراً، ABCs، قدّر BSA وأحِل لوحدة الحروق/العناية المركزة حسب العتبات، رعاية جروح ومحافظة على السوائل، اطلب استشارة جلدية/عيون مبكراً.  
- Eczema herpeticum: ابدأ acyclovir جهازي مبكراً؛ الحالات الشديدة تستدعي IV acyclovir ودخول المستشفى.  
- لا تُجرّب تنظيف/استئصال PG دون إرشاد جلدّي، أوقف الدواء المشتبه في SCARs.

5. Drugs, procedures, disposition, and follow-up
Drugs (well‑established thresholds/doses)
- IV acyclovir for severe HSV/eczema herpeticum: commonly 10 mg/kg IV q8h (adjust renal). (Standard practice; verify local protocols.)
- Doxycycline for erythema migrans (adult): 100 mg PO twice daily (duration per guideline) [NICE CKS].
- Topical impetigo first‑line: hydrogen peroxide 1% cream [NICE CKS]. Alternatives: topical fusidic acid or mupirocin per formulary.
- Immunomodulatory agents in SJS/TEN (IVIG, systemic steroids, cyclosporine): used variably; specialist decision required.

Procedures
- Estimate BSA (palm ≈ 1%). Use rule of nines for adults where helpful.
- Skin swab/culture for purulent lesions; KOH for suspected tinea.
- Skin biopsy (histology, direct immunofluorescence) for bullous disorders/unclear diagnosis.
- Avoid surgical debridement in pyoderma gangrenosum unless directed.
- Wound care: non‑adhesive sterile dressings; preserve slough in SJS/TEN per burn‑unit practice.

Disposition and admission criteria
- Admit to ward: extensive rash with moderate systemic features, facial/hand/foot/genital mucosal involvement without airway threat, significant pain or inability to care at home.
- High‑dependency / burns unit / ICU: SJS/TEN >10% BSA (high‑dependency), >30% (burns/ICU) or airway compromise, sepsis, multi‑organ failure.
- Dermatology/ophthalmology follow‑up for SJS/TEN even after discharge (ocular sequelae common).
- Admit eczema herpeticum severe/disseminated or immunocompromised for IV antivirals; outpatient oral antivirals if mild and safe at home.
- Follow-up for dermatomyositis: check CK and malignancy screening; coordinate with rheumatology/oncology.
- DRESS: arrange specialist follow-up and prolonged monitoring of LFTs/renal function (organ involvement can be delayed).

Follow-up testing
- In DRESS: serial LFTs/renal tests, CBC for lymphopenia/eosinophilia.
- In bullous autoimmune disease: autoimmune serology/biopsy, long‑term immunosuppression planning.
- In Lyme disease: clinical review at end of therapy and if new systemic symptoms.

الشرح بالعربية
- Aciclovir IV 10 mg/kg q8h شائع للحالات الشديدة (اضبط حسب الكلى).  
- دوكسيسيكلين 100 mg مرتين يومياً للـerythema migrans (اتبع مدة المحلية).  
- خزعة جلدية ومسوحات البكتيريا مفيدة للتشخيص؛ لا تنظف قرحات PG جراحياً.  
- حدد الإحالة لوحدة الحروق/العناية المركزة حسب نسبة BSA ومظاهر فشل الأعضاء.

6. High-yield FRCEM exam tricks and common pitfalls
Exam tricks
- Palm ≈ 1% BSA — quick check to stage SJS/TEN; rule of nines for larger estimates.
- Timing window for drug reactions: AGEP (within 48 h), SJS/TEN (≈1–3 weeks), DRESS (2–6 weeks) — use to identify culprit drug.
- Look for mucosal involvement and epidermal detachment percent to separate EM from SJS/TEN.
- Nikolsky positive suggests epidermal cleavage (pemphigus, TEN) — correlate clinically.

Common pitfalls
- Mistaking pyoderma gangrenosum for infective ulcer and debriding it — causes rapid worsening (pathergy).
- Overlooking systemic signs in a “morbilliform” rash — SCARs can present initially as bland exanthem.
- Routine automatic use of IVIG or systemic steroids for TEN — evidence mixed; always seek local/burn unit guidance.
- Assuming absence of fever excludes severe disease — early SJS/TEN or immunosuppressed patients may be afebrile.
- Not adjusting acyclovir dose for renal impairment — risk of toxicity.

النقاط السريعة بالعربية (نصائح للامتحان)
- استخدم كف اليد لتقدير % المساحة؛ تذكّر نوافذ زمنية للتمييز بين AGEP/DRESS/SJS.  
- لا تجرّب استئصال PG؛ لا تفترض فاعلية IVIG أو الستيرويدات في TEN دون استشارة.

7. Arabic rapid-review summary
- صنّف الطفح حسب الشكل، التوزيع، الوقت والأعراض الجهازية؛ مشاركة المخاطيات أو انفصال الجلد يتطلب تقييم عاجل.  
- SJS <10%، SJS/TEN 10–30%، TEN >30% — أوقف الدواء فوراً، قدّر BSA، وفكر في إحالة لوحدة الحروق/العناية المركزة.  
- ابدأ acyclovir جهازي مبكراً في eczema herpeticum؛ استخدم IV acyclovir في الحالات الشديدة (10 mg/kg q8h مع تعديل كلوي).  
- لا تنظف أو تزيّل قرحات pyoderma gangrenosum دون استشارة جلدية.  
- استخدم SCORTEN لتقدير خطر الوفاة في SJS/TEN واطلب استشارة متخصصة مبكراً.  
(مذكرة مراجعة سريعة؛ ليست بديلاً عن إرشادات محلية أو استشارة طبية مخصصة.)

8. Question coverage map (concepts covered)
- SCAR classification and acute management: SJS/TEN BSA thresholds, immediate actions, SCORTEN prognostic variables. (Concepts covered)
- Drug timing windows for AGEP / SJS-TEN / DRESS and their distinguishing features. (Concepts covered)
- Eczema herpeticum recognition and antiviral dosing/indications for IV therapy and admission. (Concepts covered)
- Impetigo management: topical hydrogen peroxide 1% (NICE CKS) and indications for systemic antibiotics. (Concepts covered)
- Erythema migrans (Lyme) recognition and doxycycline adult dosing. (Concepts covered)
- Bullous disorders: clinical differences pemphigus vs pemphigoid, biopsy/serology role. (Concepts covered)
- Pyoderma gangrenosum diagnosis, pathergy, contraindication to debridement, immunosuppression role. (Concepts covered)
- Diagnostic approach in ED: ABCs, BSA estimate (palm ≈ 1%), bedside tests (KOH, swabs, biopsy), baseline labs. (Concepts covered)
- Wound care principles and disposition criteria (ward vs HDU vs burns/ICU). (Concepts covered)
- Exam tricks and common pitfalls (palm rule, timing windows, pathergy, misclassification). (Concepts covered)
(Note: specific source question numbers were not supplied with the chunk notes; the map lists concepts addressed for cross‑referencing to exam question banks.)

Sources to verify
- NICE Clinical Knowledge Summaries (CKS) — impetigo, erythema migrans/Lyme.  
- DermNetNZ — AGEP and other eruption summaries.  
- SCORTEN original descriptions / burn‑unit protocols (for prognostication in SJS/TEN).  
- Regional/local formularies and specialist (dermatology/burns/ICU/ophthalmology) protocols for IVIG, corticosteroids, cyclosporine use in SJS/TEN and dosing adjustments.  
- Royal College of Emergency Medicine (RCEM), European Resuscitation Council (ERC), national dermatology and infectious disease guidelines for final verification and local practice adjustments.

(Study material only — not patient‑specific treatment advice.)


============================================================================================
CHAPTER 31: CARDIOLOGY
============================================================================================

Chapter 31: Cardiology — final chapter (compact FRCEM study notes)

1. Clinical overview and pathophysiology
- Core principle: haemodynamic state dictates urgency — unstable arrhythmia → immediate electrical therapy (synchronised DC cardioversion/defibrillation); stable → targeted pharmacology or vagal manoeuvres. [Resus Council UK 2021]
- Arrhythmia taxonomy: classify by rate (brady/tachy), QRS width (narrow/wide), regularity (regular/irregular), and stability (stable/unstable).
- Common mechanisms: re‑entry (AVNRT/AVRT/VT), enhanced automaticity (sinus tachy/brady), triggered activity (torsades from prolonged QT).
- Ischaemia/infarction causes: acute occlusion → electrical instability (VT/VF, bradyarrhythmias, conduction blocks); STEMI equivalents (De Winter, posterior MI) behave like occlusion.
- Conduction disease: Mobitz II, high‑grade AV block, trifascicular block can progress to asystole — pacing likely required.
- Structural/inherited disease: HOCM, ARVC, Brugada → exertional syncope, sudden death risk; ECG clues (HOCM: LVH/abnormal Qs; ARVC: T‑wave inversion V1–V3; Brugada: coved V1–V2).
- Devices and mechanical support: ICDs give shocks; LVADs may abolish palpable pulses (use Doppler MAP). Device interrogation is diagnostic/therapeutic.

Arabic concise:
المبدأ: شدة حالة المريض تحدد التدخّل—عدم الاستقرار → علاج كهربائى فورى؛ التوصيل: تصنيف حسب السرعة، عرض QRS، انتظام، واستقرار. اعتبارات خاصة: أمراض بنيوية/أجهزة مزروعة تغير العرض السريرى وطرق القياس.

2. Recognition, differential diagnosis, and red flags
- Red flags (urgent cardioversion/intervention): hypotension, shock, severe chest pain, acute pulmonary oedema, decreased conscious level, syncope with high‑risk ECG.
- Tachycardia patterns:
  - Regular narrow complex (HR ~150–220): likely AVNRT/AVRT (SVT).
  - Irregular wide complex: consider pre‑excited AF (WPW) vs polymorphic VT.
  - Broad regular tachycardia: VT until proven otherwise.
- Bradycardia causes and red features: symptomatic bradycardia (syncope, hypotension, ischaemia) → consider AV block, drug effect (BB/CCB/digoxin), hyperkalaemia, inferior MI.
- STEMI and equivalents red signs: V4R ST elevation → RV infarct (avoid nitrates); De Winter pattern/Wellens/backwards changes → treat as LAD occlusion.
- Pericardial tamponade: hypotension + raised JVP + muffled heart sounds (Beck’s triad), electrical alternans, low voltage ECG — urgent echo and pericardiocentesis if unstable.
- Device/LVAD flags: repeated ICD shocks, absent pulse on LVAD — do not assume no perfusion; call VAD/device team.
- High‑risk conduction disease: bifascicular/trifascicular block with syncope → urgent pacing/permanent pacemaker consideration.

Arabic concise:
مؤشرات الخطر: هبوط ضغط، وذمة رئوية حادة، إغماء؛ أنماط ECG تحدد التشخيص المبدئى (VT عند QRS واسع منتظم، WPW إذا رجفان أذيني واسع وغير منتظم). التامبوناد والجهاز/الـLVAD يتطلبان استجابة فورية خاصة.

3. Diagnostic approach and interpretation
- Immediate actions: ABCs, continuous monitor, 12‑lead ECG within 10 min, capillary glucose, IV access, oxygen if hypoxic.
- ECG classification algorithm: stable vs unstable → narrow/wide → regular/irregular → choose vagal/adenosine, antiarrhythmic, or cardioversion.
- Bradycardia work‑up: check drugs, electrolytes (K+, Mg2+, Ca2+), TSH, ischaemia; identify block type on ECG (1°, Mobitz I vs II, complete).
- Pacing capture confirmation: first electrical capture on monitor (pacing spikes + QRS change), then mechanical capture — palpate central pulse (carotid/femoral) or measure MAP. Electrical ≠ mechanical.
- WPW identification: short PR, delta wave, wide QRS; in AF with wide irregular QRS, avoid AV‑nodal blockers.
- STEMI localisation: V4R for RV infarct; posterior MI suspected when tall R / ST depression in V1–V3 → record posterior leads V7–V9; high lateral STE in I/aVL ± V2 suggests diagonal/high lateral occlusion; aVR elevation with global depression → left main/proximal severe disease.
- QTc: use Bazett’s formula (exam context): QTc = QT / √RR (s); QTc >500 ms = increased TdP risk.
- Bedside echo: tamponade, large effusion, RV dysfunction, severe LV dysfunction, mechanical complications.
- Labs: troponin, electrolytes, Mg/Ca, blood cultures x3 for suspected infective endocarditis (before antibiotics if feasible).

Arabic concise:
النهج التشخيصى: ECG فوري، تحري الأسباب القابلة للعكس (أدوية، شوارد)، تأكيد الالتقاط الكهربى والميكانيكى عند التحفيز، تسجيل قيادات خلفية (V7–V9) للـMI الخلفى، حساب QTc باستخدام بازيت فى الامتحان.

4. Emergency management algorithms
- General principle: unstable = electrical therapy first; stable = targeted pharmacology/ manoeuvres.
- Symptomatic bradycardia algorithm:
  1. Atropine 0.5 mg IV bolus; repeat q3–5 min to max 3 mg. [Resus Council UK 2021]
  2. If inadequate and unstable → immediate transcutaneous pacing (prepare pads, sedation if time), check electrical then mechanical capture.
  3. If pacing unavailable or as a bridge → isoprenaline (where local practice) or dopamine/epinephrine infusion while organising transvenous pacing. Correct reversible causes.
  - Pitfall: atropine often ineffective in high‑degree AV block — do not delay pacing.
- Transcutaneous pacing troubleshooting:
  - Start at moderate mA, increase until electrical capture then confirm pulse; if no capture at max, reposition pads (AP), check contacts, treat hyperkalaemia, arrange transvenous pacing.
- Narrow‑complex SVT (stable):
  1. Modified Valsalva manoeuvre (semi‑recumbent + leg lift) first (REVERT trial 2015).
  2. If fails → adenosine 6 mg rapid IV bolus; if no effect 12 mg (then 18 mg if local practice).
  3. Unstable → synchronised DC cardioversion (~100 J for SVT; follow device guidance).
- Pre‑excited AF (WPW + AF):
  - Never give AV‑nodal blockers (beta‑blockers, non‑DHP CCBs, digoxin, adenosine) — risk of rapid accessory pathway conduction/VF.
  - Stable → IV procainamide if available.
  - Unstable → immediate synchronised DC cardioversion. (WPW teaching; Resus guidance)
